CkU EL TEMPTATION.

Florence Hodgkinson,

Author of "TINSEL AND GOLD," Cc., SC.!

CFIAP lER XII.

"LORD ALANDALE never forgot that summer afternoon, the large roughly firnisl. -d room with its whitewashed walls and raft red roof. the surging cloud of faces, most of them familiar to him as his olvn, he thought it tiles t surely be an evil dream from which presen!),v he would awake, it could not surely be serious fact that he, the most popular man for miles round, was suspected of such an awful crime as murder.

The constible Dobbs and the gamekeeper, Body, were the first two witnesses. They de- posed to finding the body of the deceased and to both feeling a doubt whether life still lingered, they took him to Ivy Cottage and Dobbs never left him till Dr.. Gtaham .arrived. Body further stated that he had searched the ground for several yards round the spot where they found Norton 'but without finding any sort of weapon. Dr. Graham followed, depos- ing that the man was quite dead when he saw him at two o'clock, but the remains were still warm ; he should say that the fatal shot was fired between eleven and twelve, and that the victim had lingered for an hour or even two. The bullet had entered at the back which proved it could not have been a case of suicide. He was positive that no medical aid could have prplonged life, for the spine was injured; he could not say whether the man had suffered after the fatal shot; he was inclined to think that -stupor intervened and he felt nothing, but he would not be positive; he knew the de- ceased by sight, and had heard of him as a hard drinker and a man of foul and abusive language, had more than orve expressed the opinion the village would be well rid of him." Lord Alandale felt grateful to tile doctor for his last words. He knew perfectly that the kind-hearted surgeon 'had spoken them prove to the coroner and room at large that It was quite possible to want to get rid of Nor- ton without murdering him. But the words missed their point; it was very, very different everyone felt, to wish the village free of Nor- ton, from telling the dead man it would be no crime to rid the world of him. Mrs. Martin, of course, had been summoned, and she was asked particulars as to her lodger's friends apd associates who came to see him, and whether he appeased on friendly terms with them ; she was specially ques- tioned as to whether Lord Alandale called on Norton the previous day and what his busi- n,ess was ; did she hear any threat addressed to her lodger by the Earl?

At that moment Mr. Fielding stepped for- ward.

" I am here, sir," he said quietly, "-to watch the proceedings in the interests of Lord Alandale. The Earl himself is present and ready to be called as witness."

The coroner intimated that he did not desire to examine Lord Alandale at present, and the enquiry dragged on. Martha Martin and Police Constable Dobbs both testified to the threats they had overheard, the (former stat- ing in reply to a question that Lord Alandale was displeased at Norton's boasting of his ac- quaintance with Miss /Jardine, the Earl's cousin.

What Kenneth had feared all along .followed and Helen herself was called. . White as death the beautiful heiress stepped forward; she looked so ill that she was given a chair, and the questions put to her were so worded as to give her as little pain as possible.' She was. indeed, only asked ?what she knew of the dead man and if she could tell his object in coming to Alandale. She was so • nervous that in the pimple matter of giving her name her voice failed her and her answer had to be repeated in a louder voice by the coroner's clerk who stood near her. This initial ditli culty got over, she said the deceased was the husband of her deare.,t friend, Hildred Jasper, and that it was for the latter's sake she had met him and assisted him with money. She believed his sole errand at Alandale was to get pecuniary help from herself, but she began tc feel she had made a mistake in giving hirr money, for he soon demanded as a right what she had given as a charity. She grew seriously troubled as his demands upon her in- creased, and hearing from her cousin, Lord Alandale, that her meetings with Norton were ' being discussed and criticised she asked the Earl to go to him and insist on his leaving lice

in peace. So far from treating Norton harshly, the heiress went on, Lord Alandale had arranged to give him a hundred pounds to enable him to make a fresh start in life, and in two days' time he was to have left Yorkshire.

Undoubtedly Miss Jardine's evidence had done something in Kenneth's favour. If she had been an English girl those secret meetings with Norton would have told against her in every man's mind, but the people „assembled at the "Green Man" were the majority of them as hazy in their views of Colonial society as Helen's maid, Susan ; all that seemed strange in Miss Jardine's conduct they put down to her having been born and brought up in the greater empire of England beyond the seas, and they admired her frankness, only thinking she had been far too generous to the unworthy man whom she had befriended.

Only one creature in that crowed room noticed a flaw in Helen's evidence, and, that was Lord Alandale.

When he heard her tell of his promise to give Norton a hundred pounds, and that the latter had agreed to take it and leave Alandale in two days' time, Kenneth remembered that he had not seen her alone since his call on Nor- ton, and so had had no chance to tell her the result of :.his; mission. No one knew of the offer and its condition except himself and Norton, so she must have met the man once again, and that only shortly before his death. For the love of Heaven Kenneth prayed this last fact might never get abroad. If it did the burden of suspicion would be lifted from him and transferred to Helen. She was young and beautiful, 'the owner of a grand old name, and the mistress of vast wealth, but for all that if suspicion Were ever attracted to her he knew perfectly that a terrible case could be made out against her.

At last, when the enquiry was almost over, Lord Alandale was allowed to give his testi- mony, but, alas ! his friends felt it rather injured his cause than improved it, for he ad- mitted using the threats Martha Martin and the policeman had overheard; he acknow- ledged that he parted with Norton on ill terms and added the entirely unnecessary informa- tion that the man was one of the biggest scoundrels living, and that for his part he thought his fate amply merited. The jury took umbrage at this speech. The dead man was poor, he had been a convict, his life was certainly a failure, yet the twelve good men and true felt more in sympathy with him than with Lord Alandale ; they did not like to hear it said he deserved his fate. N There • was a touch of acrimony in the Earl's tone which roused them, and the enquiry resulted, as Mr. Fielding had feared for the last half hour, in a verdict of wilful murder against Kenneth Jar- dine, Earl of Alandale. 4 , Dobbs came forward with the, warrant. In an instant Mr. Fielding and the Rector were on their feet, the one word in both their mouths " Bail."

" You can talk to the magistrates about that to-morrow," returned Mr. Carter ; " it is not in my province, but I don't expect they will listen to you, as bail is never allowed in a murder case.",

"I believe in it as I believe,in my own," she answered passionately. "You no more killed Richard Norton than I did."

Had she read the yearning agony at his heart and contrived to 'give him the satisfac- tion he desired ? Did she know that he had guessed from her evidence that she saw Dick Norton at their old meeting-place last night, and that a fear had seized him lest she might have been tried, beyond her strength? If so, she had found an answer to his fears and set them at rest for ever. " I believe in your innocence as I do in my own," simple words, but they represented a double truth, they assured Lord -Mandale that she believed in him and told him that she was innocent even as himself,

CHAPTER XHI,

I WHEN ' Charles Gresham proposed to Helen Jardine tar away in Melbourne he specially told her that his coming voyage would be a short one, and that in six months' time he should return .and claim her for his wife. But things happened very differently from what he had expected ; the " Albatross " was dis- abled in a gale before she had been six weeks Out; the vessel was so terribly injured by the , ravages of the storm that she was pronounced unseaworthy and unfit for use until she had been thoroughly repaired, meanwhile her cargo was trans-shipped to another steamer belong; ing to the same owners and her crew paid off. But for his engagement to Helen Jardine young Gresham would have taken another billet as first mate at -the nearest large port and not troubled himself to return to Mel- bourne, but the thought of the girl he loved was too much temptation to him; he decided to go home and persuade Helen if possible to let their engagement be a very • brief one so that the wedding and honeymoon might inter- vene before he took another ship. Charles was her only .child, and all she had •would eventually be his. so that if he chose to remain on shore three months or even \ six there would be no danger of means running short ; he would have scorned to live entirely on his mother, but he was not in the position of a man dependent for daily bread and shelter. on his own earnings. Many cases of painful skin disease can be traced back to some illness that has left the system in a weakened state. _

Another doctor said I would have to go to the infirmary. SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

Balloon soundings have shewn surprisingly low temperatures in the upper air, 122 deg. below zero has been recorded at 48,544 ft.; while at Vienna, M. Nimfulir obtained a record of 121 deg. below zero at only 51,8721 t.

A SCIENTIFIC NOVELTY. Two weights suspended from ' a bar form a scientific novelty. When one weight is pulled , down and released it vibrates up and down for . a little time, when it suddenly stops and the • second weight beings to bob up and down. This ' soon stops, the first then resuming its motion.

===== 0002601_19060303_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
HEIGHT OF AURORA BOREALIS

The height of the aurora borealis 'seems to vary greatly. The spectral light has been seen touching the earth in Arctic regions, but measurements made during a recent aurora near London shew that the red could not have been much less than 600 miles high, the green being much "nearer the earth's surface.

===== 0002601_19060303_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW MANY ODOURS DO YOU KNOW?

People do not know as many odours as they auppose. In a recent test, 255 girls, with a set of ,twenty common odours, chiefly essential oils, only 672, on the average, were correctly named. Those most often identified were camphor, peppermint, vanilla, and cloves; those least re- cognised were hemlock, bergamot, assafoetida, wormwood, • and lavender. 1

===== 0002601_19060303_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
PLANTS THAT SEE.

Dr. Henry Conrad, Professor of Botany at John Hopkins University, declares that his ex- periments shew that plants in some forms possess all the senses of animals except that of hearing. He says: "We have been unable to discover any other way in which they are susceptible to sound, but in seeing, feeling, and tasting they are developed. The sun-dew plant, which grows in swamps around Baltimore, has the sense of taste to a greater extent than any other."

===== 0002601_19060303_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BODY AND ELECTRIC SHOCKS

The human body varies greatly in its resistance to electric shocks, and healthy men have been killed by a little more than 100 volts, while other men have survived many thousand 'volts. Nervous affections, heart disease, intemperance, and other morbid conditions increase the • dan- ger. At a late congress of Austrian medical men the conduSiont was reached from the experi- ence of recent years that death from electricity results from paralysis of the central organs of respiration, and is a special form of suffocation.

===== 0002601_19060303_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
A NEW GRAVITATION THEORY.

Gravitation is explained, says Science Siftings, by a new theory as due to the motion of matter having the tenuity usually assigned to the ether. All matter is in motion,' and the smaller the ag- gregation the greater is the Maximum velocity. Open space contains very minute particles in rapid motion in all directions. The particles are small enough to pass between the molecules of ordinary bodies—and they are stopped or re- flected n proportion to the mass of the body bombarded. Two bodies in space shield each other on one side, being thus apparently at- tracted.

ELECTRICITY AND LEAD WATER PIPES. A curious case of lead poisoning is reported to have been due to the leakage from an electric main into the water supply of a cottage. Pure soft water containing oxygen dissolves a certain amount of lead, and hence it is unsafe to store such water in leaden cisterns, or to convey it by lead pipes. With ordinary spring water, how- ever, which always possesses a certain amount of hardness, there is not this danger. The calcium sulphate io such water causes a deposit of in- soluble lead sulphate on the pipe or cistern, which is thus protected against further action. Hence lead pipes can be safely used for an ordinary water supply. A current of electricity, however, in this case with a pressure of .18 volts. passing through the water alters the chemical conditions, and enables the water to take up an appreciable quantity of lead. SPECULATIONS ABOUT RADIUM.

- - - ------- If the earth, Professor E. Rutherford tells us, had been composed,. initially, of pure radium, the activity 20,000 years later would not be greater than the activity observed in pitchblende to-day. Since, however, there is no doubt that the earth's age vastly exceeds 20,000 years, it is necessary, in order to account for the existence of radium at all in the earth, to suppose that it is continuously produced from some other sub- stance. At the present time it seems most pro- bable that uranium is the parent substance. The observed activity in a good specimen of pitch- blende is about what should be expected if the uranium contained in it spontaneously breaks up into radium. Radium itself is continuously transformed into an emanation which, in turn, is changed into other types of matter, and there is no evidence that the process is reversible.

===== 0002601_19060303_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
DIAMONDS FROM MOLTEN CARBON.

Professor R. Threlfall, in the course of an ad- dress to the Birmingham section of the Institu- tion of Electrical Engineers, treating of "Some Problems of Electro and lectro-thermal Chemistry," described the apparat used by Dr. j Ludwig in an attempt to prod e diamonds on a large scale. The apparatus Consisted of com- pression vessels and compression pumps, enabl- ing carbon to be kept under high pressure. The i carbon was heated electrically n compressed hydrogen, and it was found necessary to cool the product of the operation very suddenly, which was accomplished by injecting water at great pressure, so that the carbon was struck by water moving at immense velocities. The result of one of the experiments was to produce a sort of carbon glass, for the rapid cooling did not affori tinie for the formation of crystals of appreciable size, and the glass tended to resemble black diamonds rather than clear cubic crystals.

===== 0002601_19060303_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
HIGH FREQUENCY CURRENTS.

In an address delivered before the Society of Arts, Dr. J. A. Fleming, F.R.S., declared that a principal property of high frequency currents was that they were entirely on the surface of the conductors. It was very important to study spark resistance. A method of spark counting, which he had devised, had proved of great ser- vice and had thrown fresh light on certain in- vestigations. Spark resistanoe was a very com- plex thing, depending upon many things, of which the chief were the capacity of the jar, the inductance of the circuit, the length of the spark, and the t shape of the spark terminals. Spark resistance had been measured in another manner by Professor Slaby, at Charlottenburg, and his experiments shewed that with increase of spark length, and with increase of capacity, there was a rise of resistance. Ho himself had found that the material of which the spark balls were composed had a marked effect on spark re- sistance, iron giving a higher resistance than zinc or brass. It was also notable how great was the effect of length of spark upon resistance, as had also been demonstrated by Slaby. There were, however, many anomalies, and the work of various, investigators did not shew agreement; but the results were not necessarily contradic- tory, being rather due to the different kind of spark employed.

===== 0002601_19060303_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GREAT ICE AGE.

In a fascinating exposition--of the astronomical theory of the great Ice Age, Sir Robert Ball states that 80,000 years ago there was a severe winter. The snow was piled up in the mountain districts of Scotland, in Wales, and in the North of England. There was so much snow, indeed, that the summer sun was not able to melt it, and when the next wintor arrived and more snow fell the accumulation became greater than ever. That went on for years and years, for centuries and centuries, indeed. Yet still the snow fell, and the pressure of it gradually forced the mass to the valleys, where it was trans- formed into ice—into glaciers. Still the snow fell; the valleys got full, and the ice spread over the land, until at last a large portion a England was covered with a sheet of ice and i snow, much as Greenland s covered to-day. Before

THIS ICY 'INVASION roan fled. Then came a change. The severity of the winter began to abate. Gradually the ice and snow began to melt, and finally it dis- appeared. There were many boulders to be seen in various parts of the British Isles, which had been deposited by the ice sheet in the places where they now rest. The fact was that the planets Jupiter and Venus gradually altered the circular revolution of the earth round the sun to an oval, the prm cess taking thousands of years to accomplish, during which period the earth's track, opened out to oval form and • dos:A again to nearly circular, as though it breathed, each breath hav- ing occupied ages. Eighty thousand years ago the track of the earth was oval. The sun was not in the middle of the track, but near to one end of it, thus dividing the oval into two very un- equal parts. When the earth was speeding round the short end, so to speak, near the sun, it was summer: when going round the long end, slowly. 80 slowly, it WSS winter. Anil in

THAT TERRIBLE JOURNEY . away. from‘the sun to the far end of the elipse the hemisphere turned away from the' source of light and kept accumulating more ice and snow. The brief summers failed to melt it. and so the great ice cap was tormed, and its duratiOtl now called the Ice Age. Sir Robert Ball adds that another Ice Ago will come on the emit' ill 200,000 zera' time.

MADE YOUNG AGAIN.

Iron-Worker Cured of Chronic Indigestion by Bile Beans.

Mr. Joseph H. Richmond, an iron-worker, who lives at 45, Elizabeth Ann-street, Manchester, is now a young- looking and vigorous man of 34, capable of doing a hard day's work with ease. A WOMAN in New Orleans, suddenly made a widow by a recent railroad accident, mint to an agent to obtain the amount for which her ,husband's life was insured. The agent kindly told her that he sincerely sympathised with her in her bereavement, and was astonished by the energetic response of the excited lady, who exclaimed: "Yes! that's always the way with you men. You are very polite about everything else,,but whenever a poor woman gets a chance to make a little money you're only sorry." TIM other morning as a New Haven train was coming through the tunnel, a nervous old gentleman, who is noted for his obstinacy, thought it had taken a side track, but wts assured that it was on the central doable-track line of that tunnel. He indig- nantly spurned that idea, and imperiously wanted to know if he didn't know what he was talking about. Jiist then the Boston Express came thundering by, and the old gentleman was assailed with, "What do you say now ?" " What do I say now 7" he cried; "why, I say if another train passes us before we get out of this infernal tunnel, it—it—will shake mil confidence in my own opinion!" _

THE Hon. Thomas Keir Murray, C.M.G., who bas been chosen as Natal's representative at the forth.. comingopening of the Australian Federal Par! lament, is a Natalian, born at Pinetown. He served in the Zulu War of 1879, and recently he was on General Boiler's Staff up to the relief of Ladysmith. Bis political career dates from 1886, and he has been Colonial Secretary of his colony. He takes a great interest in farming and in sports.

Prince and Princess Christian.—King Edward was represented by King Christian at the silver wedding of the German Emperor and Empress, and at the marriage of their Majesties' second son, Prince Eitel Fritz,which took place on that anniversary. The absence of the Heir Apparent, the Duke of Connaught, and Prince Arthur of Connaught, leaves few royal Princes of suffi- cient rank upon whom the King can call to represent him, on the various occasions when it is impossible for his Majesty to appear in person, but the presence of Prince Christian in Berlin was singularly fortuitous as he is an uncle of the German Empress, and was one of the witnesses of her betrothal to the Kaiser. When at home, Prince and Princess Christian reside at Cumber- land Lodge, which, though not very large is a charm- residence near Windsor. Their onlysurviving a", Prince Albert, is serving in the Russian Army.but is a frequent 'visitor at Cumberland Lodge, where Princess Christian carries on much of her philanthropic work, and an extensive correspondence. The Ball last Norfolk at Arundel Castle.—The Duke and Duchess gave a magnificent ball at Arundel Castle week, to which moat of the country houses in West Sussex sent partiea. Since the Duke's second marriage , there have been several small entertainments at the Castle. but the ball last week was the first to be given there for'very many years. Dancing was in the Baron's Hall, ft great mediaeval which is said to be the largest room in any private house in the Kingdom, but most of the ancient castle fell into ruins after the civil war. The work of restoration was taken in hand last century, and the present Duke is responsible for the greater part of the existing Castle. Modern improvements have been introduced, but the attempt to combine these with ancient characteristics has not been wholly successful, and, according to some writers,the huge building retains Much of the gloom and discomfort of a mediaeval mtetle.

The Examiner, Saturday, March 3, 1906.

WOMAN'S REALM.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

ANGLO-AMERICAN MARRIAGES.

It has often struck me, says a writer in the Sketch, that in the Anglo- American unions which have become such a frequent and favourite habit the wife must have moments of astonishment and disillusion in realising her husband's attitude to the Eternal Feminine, whioh is so very different from that of men in the States. As a well-known traveller recently set forth as the result of many experiences, the least-considered person in the whole household is usually the man who works hard for the benefit of everyone beneath his roof. "He is very generous this American father,': the author in question goes on to say, "considerate: to his wife, kind to his children, absolutely just at his death, inasmuch as he divides his money equally between sons and daughters. He has less leisure and less fun than anyone else in his household, toiling and slaving as he does inces- santly for the benefit of others." So, altogether, the American woman has undoubtedly the best ,1 of it in every way while over there, and it 11 must be rather a revelation to see with what calm unconsciousness our men sip the cream of life, leaving the second-best of everything as the natural portion of their devoted feminine belongings. I know one American bride who raged and chafed inwardly because her husband ' did not bring her flowers on his return every evening, open tine door for her on leaving a room, and perform various petits soins by which, as a matter of course, the was sur- rounded when at home; while he, poor man, was all the time wondering at the back of his mind why shts did not keep his gloves and pipes and impedimetAta large or small in the state of miraculous readiness to which an admiring mother and sisters had long accustomed him.

CASHMERE INDOOR GOWN.

Our illustration depicts a graceful indoor gown of mauve cashmere, the skirt being ar- ranged in the becoming corselet style now SO fashionable. It is open a little at the waist in front, and laced across with narrow ribbon velvet a little darker in tone than the material. The skirt is very wide at the hem, and is

trimmed with two wide bands of velvet. The short zouare of cashmere is outlined with bands of velvet, and slashed on the shoulders to shew the full bodice of coarse lace, dyed to match the material. The sleeves are also of lace, and gathered into a band of velvet at the elbow, finishing with small tabs edged with velvet and a frill of the lace.

FUR AS AN ADORNMENT

We never realise the extreme beauty and be- comingness of fur till we see it in juxtaposition to some elegant fabric on gown or mantle. Fur is always the mode. Which of us can over re- member a time when it was not worn? But just now its uses are more expanded, and a vast variety of skins aro in demand as apparel in some way or other. In fashionable life fur is most assertive; capes, boas, and stoles all grow larger, and with them toques are worn to cor- respond, and bands of fur are introduced on to cloth and velvet gowns for day wear, and on to much lighter fabrics for the evening. Sable, ermine, and mink are most to the Pore, bu i t there s a great feeling for chinchilla, which ac- cords so well with the grey tones now so much in vogue. Perhaps this use of fur generally is the more notable, says the Queen, because, what- e-er we may have to find fault with, the winter is certainly a mild one. Thp only wonder is how, in what are considered hard times, so many women can afford to lock up many hundreds Of pounds in the long sable and sealskin coats you see on all sides leaving any ball-room or any fashionable theatre. There is a great variety in the shapes; long capes, short capes, sweeping sable coats reaching to the hem of the skirt, with wide important sleeves; semi-fitting short and long coats, especially in Persian lamb. Some have wide collars, some are liberally trimmed with lace, but few have stand-up collars, for, however comfortable, they no longer have the cachet of fashion. CHARMING SHIRT BLOUSE.

With the approach of spring plain shirt blouses become ever-increasingly popular. , A very charming example of such a blouse is- illus- trated in our sketch. The material used for its achievement is a fine white delaine with a nar- row heliotrope stripe in its weave. Down the front of the blouse runs a very wide box-pleat, adorned ori its outer edges with sows of

n-achine-stitching in heliotrope silk. Beyond this box-pleat are arranged on either side seven or eight buttons covered with heliotrope silk The upper part of the shirt has a novel ar- rangement of 'straps of the material, finished with rows of machine-stitching, and further decorated with large heliotrope buttons. The sleeves are of the modified bishop shape, and terminate in neat little cuffs stitched with heliotrope silk: 'A smart cravat of heliotrope ribbon, finished with a white turn-over collar, completes the neat and pretty blouse. HAND-PAINTED CORSETS. Hand-painted silk corsets form the latest acqui• sition of the eleE,,ante, and the most lovely ex- amples are to be seen in pale coral pink taffetas covered with a design of faded roses, or in the most delicate shade of mauve, the pattern of shaded lilac being carried out in mauve and white.

WARRINGTON.

Personal. Mr. J. F. Awbery, Master of the Warrington Work- house, on Saturday presided over a meeting of the members of the National Association of Workhouse Masters of England and Wales, which was held in the Lord Mayor's Parlour, Town Hall, Manchester. Mr. Awbery is president of the Association. On Saturday Grand Master David Houghton, of the Order of Druids, visited the Stockton Equalized Dis- trict of the Order of Druids. As a memento of the visit, Bro. J. Eaton, P.G.M., presented Mrs. Houghton, wife of the Grand Master, with a silver teapot and tray.

Up-to-date Fire Places.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge et., Warrington.—Advt. Crosfleld v. the Ship Canal Company. The Court of Appeal, on Saturday, on the applica- tion of Mr. 'Cripps, K.C., fixed Wednesday, the 14th of March, for settling the minutes of the judgment in the case of Croslield v. the Manchester Ship Canal Company. Mr. Cripps also obtained leave to serve the company with notice of motion on a question of costs for that day.

ASK for CUNNINGHAM'S HIGH-CLASS MINERAL WATERS.—Advt. St. Mary's Dance The-annual dance in connection with St. Mary's (R.C.) Church, Latchford, was held at the Parr Hall on Monday evening and was in every way a success. About 350 persons were present and dancing was kept up with zest from 8 p.m. till 2 a.m, to music supplied by Mr. A. Starkey's excellent band. The room and coiridors had been tastefully decorated for the occasion, and the arrangements which were excellent reflected great credit on the committees, with Mr. G. T. Bibby as hon. secretary. • 3, ft. WOOD, Piano Dealer and Tiiner,Friars Gatp, and 47, Old London-road., Stockton Heath.—Advt. Warrington Entertainers at Crowton. A Party of Warrington artistes gave a successful concert in connection with Crowton Primitive Methodist Chapel on wennesday evening. There was a large attendance,Conncillor James Evans (Warring- ton), presiding. An enjoyable programme was con- tributed to by Miss Alice Woodland (soprano). Miss Lily Fairhurst (contralto), Mr. J. Willett (tenor), Mr. Percy J. George (baritone), Master Tom Barton, R.A.M. (violinist), Mr. E. A. Sanders (humourist), and the .Lyric Quartette Party. All the items were well received, the efforts of the artistes meeting with the warmest appreciation. • ASK for CUNHINGHS.M'S HIGH-CLASb lilmanAL WATERS.—Advt. Warrington P.S.A. Society. The weekly meeting of the Warrington P.S.A. Society was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey- street, when Mr. J. Hamblett presided over a good attendance. An address on " Friendship " was delivered by Mr. E. Lloyd Jones, who stated that a London paper had recently offered a prize for the best definition of a friend, and the definition that was con- sidered the best was—" A true friend is one who comes in when everybody else has gone out." He proceeded to point out what an important part the choice of friends played in the formation of character, and made an earnest appeal to all present to make Jesus Christ their friend and guide. Two solos that were greatly appreciated, were rendered by Miss Winnie Jones, of Stockton Heath. Mr Rowland Hill was the accompanist. Councillor W. Clegg read the scripture lesson. The president also took part in the service.

The monthly meeting in connection with the Ebeneer Church Band of Hope Society was held in the church on Monday evening and was well attended. Mr. H. Thornton, who was in the chair, gave an address to the children and young people, and it was followed by a recitation entitled " The miller's boy," by Mr. A. Hughes. The church organist gate a solo on the piano entitled" Fire, fire," and ai action song was rendered by Miss J. Hughes, Miss Aikinhead and others, the artistes being dressed in appropriate costumes. Miss Nellie Hart recited "I will marry no man if he drinks," and Miss E. Lee sang two solos. Another action song " Nellie's Dolly" was given by seven very small girls. Miss Jenny Hughes followed with a recitation and Harry Richardson gave "Tom Jones," Miss Jones a song, Mr. Jackson a pianoforte solo, and Miss England a song. Misses A. Dutton and Thompson favoured with a pianoforte duet " Aeross the snow," and the entertainment concluded with the song" Strike of the beer jugs," by 16 boys and girls. The meeting closed with the singing of the Doxology.

UNDER THE CLOCK for Good BOOTS.—Advt.

Junior Liberal Association. At the weekly meeting of this Association held at Gregory's Cafe, Buttermarket-street, on Monday evening three interesting biographies of eminent men were submitted by members of the biography com- mittee. —Mr. W. Williams read a pacer on Lord Granville "who filled with great distinction some of the highest offices of the State and by his tact and persuasive power was able to carry measures through the House of Lords which would have been rejected if piloted by any other man.—A brief biography of " Mr J. Passmore Edwards "was submitted by Mr. J. Haw elorn who pointed out that the subject of the paper was a successful newspaper man still amongst us who, having gathered finely of this world's wealth, had given the evening of his days a golden setting by a liberal-handed and intelligent philanthropy, which had brightened and sweetened the lives of many of his less favoured countrymen and women. Mr. Fordl read a paper on" Richard Cobden "and referred par- ticularly to the Free Trade struggle in which this great man played such a noble part. Cobden was the representative of the highest statesmanship and the true ideal of what a statesman ought to be. Living in constant and active intercourse with his fellowmen for 30 years, hemas never convicted and never suspected of trick or subterfuge, or hidden design. He was transparently pure. He laboured purely for the common, good, and sacrificed a leading commercial position to become useful to his countrymen.—A. dis- cussion followed.—On the motion of Mr. A. H. Rathbone, seconded by Mr. Derbyshire, a vote of thanks was passed to the essayists. —The meeting was presided over by Mr. H. W. Guest.

WEDDING PRESENTS.—Large Selection Of Useful and Ornamental Articles.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge at., Warringt6n.—Advt. Concert at Stockton Heath

The concert which was held on Tuesday evening I in the Hall Cafe, Assembly Room, in aid of the build- ing fund of the poor children's summer camp, was, I from a musical standpoint, a great success. It is to be regretted, however, that as regards the attendance the proceedings did not receive the amount of atten- tion which they richly deserved. The extreme inclemency of the weather probably mainly accounted for the smallness of the attendance and in this respect the promoters of the concert were most unfortunate. An excellent programme was opened by the Warring- ton Apollo Prize Male Voice Choir with the selection "Crossing the plain." Under the conductorship of Mr. Harry Berrey, the choir entered fully into the spirit of the piece which afforded admirable scope for them to show their capabilities. Well balanced, the choir was admirably represented in every department and their efforts were very well received-. Miss Alice Wbodland was evidently suffering from cold and was somewhat husky but otherwise her singing of " Dear heart ' was excellent. The Lyric Quartette (the Misses Woodland and Fairhurst, and Messrs. It. W. Cook and P. J. George) were heartily encored for a humorous item " tooth- ache ' sung to the accompaniment of suitable gestures, and in response gave an excellent rendering of " Killarney." Their efforts later in the evening met with equal success. Mr. Tom Barton contributed to the instrumental side of the entertainment with two well played violin solos in which he displayed unusual talent an an appreciation of the compositions. Miss Lily' Fairhurst was encored for the contralto solo " The Enchantress " which she sung with spirit whilst Mr. Dan Gittens gave a capable render- ing of "The deathless army. Highly acceptable items were a recital and a descriptive sketch given by Mr. Clement George whose only fault was that he was perhaps a little too dramatic. During an interval a brief address was delivered by Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins, hon. secretary of the Children's Camp,who emphasised the excellent work which was done by the movement. He pointed out that Lady Greenall had generously given £ll5 for the efection of an assembly room at the p3rmanent camp. It was desired to extend this work by erecting cubicles so that they could send adults to Ithe camp in the event of sickness.

Potroleum Oil in barrels and drums delivered free.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, bridge st., Warring- ton.—Advt.

A VISIT TO "THE HOUSE"

[By Charles B. Parker.]

Being in London I resolved to visit "The House," to which, with great effort, and with a great majority, we sent a Croafield to represent Warrington. Along , with many others anxious to see the gathering of the. Commons of England, I sent in my card to our Member, and after waiting about a quarter of an hour. Mr. Crosfield with beaming face, came and picked me I out, and hurried me into the Strangers'. Gallery. I . was followed by the envious glances of at least fifty ! strangers who had not yet found their Members. "Come along, Morley is up, you- most not miss him." said his admirer, and up the stairs We went, and found one vacant place. I was soon looking down upon the historic floor. What a change since I last looked upon this scene, the men who for many years I had seen on the Opposition benches, and in a helpless minority, were in power, backed by the biggest majority of modern times. "C.-B." was absent, but. there was Asquith, Fowler, Brice, Gladstone, Burns,. Buxton, and Birrell. Morley was up looking more. alert and stronger than when we last saw him at Manchester, when he seemed weary after the comple- tion of his life of his great chief. Something about the psrtition of Bengal was the subject. The first words I heard were" I assure the House that I shall not, so long as I have any responsi.- bility for Indian affairs, be likely to depart from those. general principles of Liberalism, &c. Lord Percy followed and speke for the late Government, who were! responsible for this partition, in question. He spoke pleasantly and with a manifest knowledge of the sub- I ject. It would be well if this scion of the great House of Percy could !get rid of the nervous habit of washing his hands in invisible' soap all the time he is. speaking. After the Bengal amendment had been by leave ' withdrawn, we had an amendment moved by Sir WI Evans Gordon, the Conservative Member for Stepney. expressing regret that His Majesty's Speech contained ' no reference to the exclusion from this country of ; foreign labour during times of trade disputes. The funny feature about this amendment was that it was practically identical with an amendment proposed by Hon. Keir Eardie, when the Aliens Bill of last season was before the House—an amendment supported by the bulk of the Liberal party, and curious to say opposed by the very member who now asked the , House to express regret that it is not included in the King's Speech. The design of, the amendment was ' only too apparent. It was intended to divide-the Labour and Liberal parties, but the trap: was too apparent. Herbert Gladstone brought out clearly the inconsistency. In seeing the son of the great commoner speaking from the same bench from which I have heard' his father hold the House 1 spelbound I could not help drawing comparisons. The Gladstone of to-day is no orator like his sire, I but a plain blunt Englishman, who knows what he means and says it in language devoid of all orna- ment, and in words everyone can understand. He hiis made his own place and will keep it. The next i speaker I could hardly see from the seat I occupied, I but seeing that all the members assumed an attitude I of expectancy I bent forward and recognised that one I of the strongest of Labour memlsers had "caught the; Speaker's eye" Will Crookes rewarded the expectant 1 members by a slashing good humoured attack on the inconsistencies of the member for Stepney. The I Bill of last session was "a farce, a make-shift for I killing time," "my Latin for it"and members listened I eagerly for Will's Latin " is that the country was I " bluffed." Crookee will always be able •to hold the i attention of the House ; it likes honesty; and like 1 the rest of mankind the members like to be amused. Crookes took the bull by the horns and told ' ' the Tories that if they carried the amendment to a division, Labour wcaild help to vote it down; but that they, the Labour members, were preparing a bill of their own on the subject, and he added slyly he expected it would' go through as an unopposed measure. He is one of those who can move a• popular audience from laughter to tears and burst again to laughter in as little time as it takes' to tell it, and in , this be is greatly aided by a voice which he modulates 1 probably unconsciously, with wonderful effect. He delivers a few sentences with the force of an eighty ton hammer, and rapidly changing the thought and the voice, in tender tones he tells of home hardships and wrong. Not being able to see the speaker, more than once I got the morcentary impression from the change of voice that another member was addressing the House. Chamberlain had come in looking (at any rate from the distance)as young as ever, and was soon on his feet."ln view of the importantlatatement made regarding the Labour members' intention of bringing in a Bill he did not think it necessary that the amendment should be pressed." We all laughed, and were of the same opinion, and at a signal from the Speaker we adjourned for dinner. I joined our Member in the lobby, and seeing my other Member, the one who represents the constituency in which I live, Mr. J. A. Seddon, the Labour Member for the Newton Division, we button-holed him. We were soon discussing together the list of the exports of the kitchen department of the House of Commons, and between the free importation of the beef, and the cheese, we settled to our entire satisfaction the " Education Difficulty" and it is a great pity that Mr. Birrell was not with us; then by the aid of coffee and cigars we defined the limits of Socialism and individualism. When this was accom- plished we separated, the Members retiring to I the House to get the address agreed to, and the writer returning to his club to write these rough impressions of his visit to the House. By the way, who is my double in the House? I never pass the Westminster Bridge crossing to Parliament Square, but the officer stops the traffic for me. This is a courtesy extended specially to Members of Parliament. I went down to the House in fear and I trembling lest my double had gone under in the great flood, but again all vehicles were held up until I was safely over. Long may my double live to represent his constituency in Parliament and to represent the writer to the "force" C.E.P.

NAVAL ESTIMATES REDUCED BY £1,520,000.

The Goveinment are endeavouring to make good their promises of economy and efficiency, and the Naval Estimates for the ensuing year show a decrease of over £1,520,000. The total sum asked for is £31,869,500, compared with £33.389,500, the amount of last year's estimates. The number of new ships to be commenced during the year is as follows :—Battleships, 4 ; ocean- going destroyers, S; coastal boat destroyers, 12 ; submarine boats, 12 ; total, 33. Of the four battleships to be commenced daring the year 1906-7 two will be built in dockyards, one at Portsmouth and one at Devonport, and two will be built by contract. In the case of all the new vessels it is stated that the design has not yet been settled.

Sensational Suicide at Wang ngton

A sensational suicide was inquired into by Coroner Brighouse and a jury at Warrington Police Station on Tuesday. It appears that James Jones, (48), wire- drawer, 3, Wright-street, was summoned to appear at the Police Court last Friday on a charge of indecent assault. As he did not attend the Court a warrant was issied for his arrest and with the object of executing it P.S. Bell accompanied by P.C.'s Burton and Reddington visited Jones's house at one o'clock on Monday morning. The sergeant was about to knock at the door when Mrs. Jones came Irma the next house and said her husband had locked her out. The officer knocked feveral times but received no answer and hearing a peculiar noise in the front bed- room he became suspicious that all was not right. Mrs. Jones broke a pane of 'glass in the kitchen window and by that means P.C. Burton effected an entrance and let the other officers in by the front door. On going upstairs a shocking speFtacle confronted them. Jones was lyinwn the bedlin the front room, divested of all his othing with the exception of his shirt. His throat was cut, and in his right hand he had a clasped table knife. Jones was unconscious, but not quite dead. When the police surgeon (Dr. Smith) arrived life was extinct.—Mrs. Jones stated that on Filday her husband appeared to be upset. He was very strange in his manner on Saturday night, and ordered her/ out of the house. ,He had been drinking rather heavily lately. He had ' never threatened to take away his life, and it was only the Police Court affair which had an effect upon his ' mind.—The jury returned a verdict of "Suicide whilst temporarily ir sane."

A record in cross-Channel traffic for this time of year h as been created during February. The numbers travell- ing by the midday turbine steamer from Dover to Calais reached as many as 400 a day, and train traffic from London has been doubled.

LOCAL EDUCATION.

Interesting Report by Mr. G. Walker.

IS THE TOWN " GETTING VALUE FOR MONEY P"

At the annual meeting of the Warrington Infirmary Workingmen's Committee on Saturday afternoon, Mr. George Walker, who represents the Committee on the local Education Authority, presented an interesting report on the year's educational work. He pointed out that in pursuance of their policy of co-ordinating the educational methods in the Borough, the Committee had secured a controlling interest in the Grammar School, and the arrangement entered into provided, in con- sideration of financial aid, for additional free scholarships and a reduction of fees. It was hoped by this means to increase the popularity and further extend the usefulness of the school to students who intended to follow a professional career. Two new schools had been sanctioned, and in one case the building was so far advanced that it was hope& it would be ready for use towards the close of the year. The cost of these schools would be very high but it must be remembered that the committee were not free agents in the matter as the schools must be built to 'suit the requirements of the Board of Education who preferred efficiency to economy and seldom sought to combine both. The estimates of the committee for the coming year slowed as usual a considerable increase in expenditure largely accounted for as in previous years by the annual increase of salaries to teaching staffs. In this respect it appeared to be one's privilege to pay and grumble. (Laughter.) If they felt assured that they received full value in return for increased expenditure they would have the satisfaction they desired, but there was a strong feeling, not confined to this town alone, that they did not. There were not wanting those who declared that the teaching was not so efficient and it was feared that the frequent changing of staffs and the 1 understaffing in some schools must be detri- mental to eiliciency. In explanation they were told that there was a scarcity of teachers and that was so But the policy of the experts who limited the number of schools available for the training of pupil teachers and fixed the high ideal which forbade the employment of article 50 teachers and would only employ certificated teachers was to some extent responsible for it. Why this town should refuse to employ teachers who were daily advertised for by other and more important authorities was difficult to appreciate. He had previ- ously asserted before that committee his belief that too many subjects were attempted in elementary schools. Officialism and faddists restricted the usefulness of the schools by attempting a curriculum of from 13 to 26 subjects to be taught in a week. That, he contended, was as unreasonable as it was impossible. There was no attempt even at variation in accordance with the spirit of the times. No wonder that work was indiffer- ently done. He was sure that good writing was almost a lost art, while the manner of holding the pen was almost as varied as the number of schools. (Laughter.) If the elementary schools taught e'ficiently fewer sub- jects, of which shorthand should be one, the scholars would be better qualified to take up workshop and office duties than they were now. The days of the three It's seemed to be past, but as one writer said : "We were still waiting in the wilderness for an educational Joshua to lead UR to the promised land of some prac- tical system." Mr. Walker expressed regret that owing to the poor circumstances of their parents many chil- dren were obliged to leave school at an early age and enter occupations ml which their education would be of no particular advantage. Those able to acquire a good education, he continued, realized the pleasures and advantages which were denied to those less favoured, but on the other hand it was a mistake to assume that even the most thorough education was the golden key to a successful business or professional career. In our workshops a good hustler was often preferred to the expert and intelligent workman and a willing worker to a profound thinkes, and such were the general labour conditions that inure was required from strength and expertness than technical knowledge. We were frequently told that we had fallen behind our competitors in trade owing to our lack of education but lan experienced American, whose name was an authority on the subject, told him that so far as twelve months' experietwe here had shown him, the fault lay with the employers rather than with the workman in their unwillingness to bring antiquated methods up to date. It was admitted that the evening continuation classes were not accomplishing all the good the committee had hoped for and they regretted the falling off in the num- bers attending the classes between the opening and the close of the session. The technical classes were main- tained at great cost and it was an open question how many studends attended hoping to gain a material advantage in business life and how many took a subject simply as a hobby or a recreation. The question of a national system of education was deserving of sad, he believed, was receiving the attention of our best minds. What was taught in our elementary schools especially should be eminently useful and as far as possible should suit the individual needs and prospects ,of the general scholar. There should be fewer restric- tions and less officialism to hamper the energies of the teachers and the greatest care should be taken to guard 'against excessive and extravagant expenditure which if carried too far might occasion a reaction they would all deplore. (Applause.) The MAYOR, who presided, said Mr. Walker deserved the thanks of the meeting for the very careful way in which he had dealt with the subject. Mr. Joirx FA.lncr.oupx, in proposing a vote of thanks, said the manufacturers of Warrington were awaking to the importance of secondary education to the working classes. At the meeting a the Chamber of Commerce it was suggested that action might be taken with a view to compelling the, boys employed by local firms to attend the evening classes during their apprenticeship. In responding to the vote of thanks Mr. WALKER said he had attended 156 meetings during the three years he had been connected with the authority and he , had always been treated with courtesy by every mem - her of the committee. He had been appointed on several committees, including the Sites and Buildings Committee, which was subject to some very severe criticisms. He was pleased to say that the Arayor was one of the strongest economists. (Hear, hear.) He at least was not on the side of extravagant expenditure and he had criticised the plans of the new schools with a view to obtaining the highest efficiency at the lowest cost. He certainly deserved the thanks of the town in I the matter. (Applause.)

THE CORNISII RIVIERA.

People as a rule do not think often of health resorts and seaside places during the winter. Instead they wait patiently for the sunny days of the slimmer holidays. Most of them are unacquainted with the fact that there is a" Cornish riviera ' which offers great attractions of climate and health-giving environment even in 'the depth of winter. These people can extend their know- ledge by reading the well-written and charmingly illus- trated bookl6t issued by the Great Western Railway Company under the title of this paragraph. They will learn that the necessity for travelling to foreign climes in order to avoid the generally trying English winter exists no longer. There is no need to leave England, I for it can be proved that the winter-climate of the I Cornish Riviera rivals that of the chief foreign health 1 resorts in the matter of mildness and surpasses them in equability. The Gulf stream has slot to do with warming this corner of the country and the temperature of the sea-water seldom falls below 4f3 degrees on the surface while the temperature of the air remains at a higher minimum than at such places as Mentone, Nice, Cannes, Montpelier, etc. In addition to its climatic advantages, the Cornish Riviera. of course, includes some of the quaintest and most charming places in the British Isles. Resorts like Falmouth, Fow,iy, Truro. Perran- porth, St. Ives and others are well known by repute.and they will be found fully described in the G.W.R. booklet. Railway facilities have been greatly improved ; in recent years and people from the North will find it a I comparatively easy matter now to run down to tho 1 South-West corner of Engtind.

AND LIAMMOND'S ItHUISIBR —The cheapest and surest method of obtaining an introduction with view to marriage, or situation as Housekeeper, Companion, Caretaker, dm. ; small registratioa fee charged ' • no other charges whatever ; stamped l address.--Ttoez tts H•MMOND, 139, Hill rd., Cambridge,

There are not many seats THE CITY that Mr. BALFOUR could ELECTION, have won whilst the nation is in its present rational temper. It was his good fortune to possess a relative who was in a position to offer him a seat that it was impossible even for him to lose. What might have happened if it had been possible to prolong what has been an exceedingly brief con- test belongs to the field of conjecture. Nobody, of course, expected Mr. GIBSON BOWLES to defeat the late Prime Minister. Mr. BOWLES did not expect it himself. The City, once extremely Radical is now overwhelmingly Tory. It once maintained a brave fight for English liberties. It stood so stoutly by Jortx WILKES, on grounds that would now be called Radical, that WILKES himself, who was a hero scarcely worthy of the occasion, became ashamed of its fervuur, and declared that he was "no Wilkesite." It erected monuments to BRASSCROSBY the Lord Mayor who defied both Parliament and the Court in behalf of the freedom of election and liberty of speech. Even in much later days it was soundly true to Liberal, or, perhaps, to speak more accurately, to Whig tradition. It returned LORD JOHN Russum, to Parliament, and was proud of having done so. But that was in the days before the City had begun to feel the f all effects of Free Trade. The tradesman then lived above his shop. He now lives in his suburban villa. Tne merchant had his residence within the square mile of the City boundaries. The stockbroker was much more frequently a resident than a person who went to the City in the morning and left it in the after- noon. There was a real constituency of residen- tial voters, where, if the actual residents were polled to-day, there would be a constitueticy of servants and caretakers. Those who elect the members for the City of London do not live there. They live at an average of thirty miles away, in anything from WHITTAKER WRIGHT palaces to 11 villas of the more modern class. They form a I rank, a class of their own, and the great majority of them support politically the class, the rank, that is above them. They are on the side of "established institutions," which means that they are bent on climbing up to what is above them in the social scale, whatever that may be. Mr. Giesox BOWLES has had an experience that he appears to have found very delightful, but has been rather a rough one on the whole. The City man has no fineness of feeling when an election is in progress. He is the schoolboy who has grown up into a man. Mr. BOWLES, who is a great yachtsman, holding a master mariner's certificate, roughed it "in the City with evident enjoyment We doubt whether Mr. BALFOUR liked his City experience quite so much. It is probable that he will like the neW House of Commons even I less. When he gets back he will find it a I House greatly changed. He and his brother Gerald rejoiced not very long ago over the bright prospect of doing as they liked with a Liberal Government. Gerald has been swept away by the tide. His elder hrother will go back to a House that, so far from not knowing him, knows him too well. He has cut a great figure there in his time. 1 We will do him the justice to say that in the last I Parliament he wielded an almost miraculous sway. But those times are all past and gone. Mr. BALFOUR may do his very best in the new House of Commons, as no doubt he will ; but he will find it singularly regardless of those arts and artifices which so conquered the mind of its prede- cessor. He will find, in fact, what he has never before known—a Parliament determined on doing business.

It was fitting that Mr. THE BOROUGH A. H. Crosfleld, M.P.,should MEMBER. preside at the powerful lecture delivered in the Parr Hall on Thursday evening by Mr.W.Madden, on "Daniel O'Connell." As is well known, the Borough Member has always had a warm sym- pathy with the political aspirations of the inhabi- tants of the Sister Isle which first found I unmistakable expression in the lives of such 1 high-minded men as Daniel O'Connell. Great interest centred in Mr: Crosfield's attend- ance, as this was the first occasion ' since his triumphant return to Parliament that he had had the opportunity of addressing the towns- ' people on political all sirs. His speech was a well-chosen ORO embodying interesting impressions of Parliamentary life and thoughtful views on the great questions which confront the Governmentit is more than gratifying to know that his short ex- perience in the House of Commons has thoroughly convinced him that the government have not the remotest intention of holding office on a mere negative policy. Great social reforms demand attention and there is an honest determination to grapple with them in a way which will benefit the mass of the people. Mr. Crosfield was right in commending the Government's courageous policy lon the South African question and, coming to home affairs, the workers will be pleased to note Mr. Crosfield's sympathetic attitude towards Labour and its representatives. The electors are justified in hoping great things from the Govern- ment, and Mr. Crosfield's constituents may be assured of his loyal support to measures of justice , and progress.

We are pleased to note that Rantrcpro PUBLIC- the Warrington Licensing HOUSES. Corn mi t tee contemplate taking steps at the adjourned Licensing Sessions on Friday next to reduce the number of licences. It was apparently with this object in view that the renewal of Ilicences was deferred from the annual Brewster Sessions and last week-end the committee mot and thoroughly discussed the matter. The remit was that instructions were given to the Chief Constable ,to give formal notice of objection to the renewal of four licences within a radius of 400 yards of , Market Gate which the committee consider unneces- sary. Under the Licensing Act the procedure to be adopted to effect this little reduction is somewhat complicated. The Chief Constable will make the objections at next Friday's sessions and if the Licensing Committee adhere to their intention they will renew the licences provisionally. The whole question will then be referred to the Cora- -1 pensation Authority, which consists of practically all the Borough magistrates, and if they agree to close the houses the amounts of compensation will have to be arranged. As there are seventy-three licences within a quarter .mile area of Market Gate —giving an average of one to about every 100 inhabitants—it is almost unnecessary to urge the desirability of a reduction. Some of the streets can indeed fitly be described as "congested areas' and the policy of the Licensing Justices in avail- ing themselves of the very limited powers con- ferred by the Act will, we venture to think, meet with the approval of all who have the welfare and advancement of the town at heart.

The freedom of the small JUVENILE SigoEEEs boy to buy " fags " when BEWARE, and where he wills is likely to be very much curtailed ere long, for Dr. Macnamara, M.P., is shortly to introduce a Bill aimed directly at juvenile smoking. The Bill provides that no person shall sell, give, or supply tobacco in any form to, or for, the use of any person under the age of sixteen years, under penalties of 203. on a first conviction, 40.0 n a second conviction, and in addition to the foregoing penalties the licence (if any) held by such person for the sale of tobacco shall in case of a third con- viction become void, and such person shall be dis- qualified for a term of five years from the date of such conviction from holding any such licence. Much of the credit for this prospective beneficent 'legislation is due to the labours of the Inter- national Anti-Cligarette League, founded five years -ago, and which has already eurolled over 65,000 boys pledged to abstain from tobacco until the age of 21, while the membership is still increasing at the rate of 400 a week. We are inclined to regret somewhat that there is no prohibition against juvenile smoking, such as obtains in some of the American States, for neither the prospective Bill nor the efforts of the League will stamp out alto- gether the baneful habit which seems to be speed- ing among the boys of the nation. Between them, however, they will certainly lessen the evil very considerably, end for that we should all be devoutly thankful.

The Cyclists Touring Club INTBRESTING TO draws attention to a recent CircusTs, case which is of interest to all cyclists and local author- ities. A Mr. Turner of Bournemouth sued the Poole Town Council for £2 2s. compensation for injuries Sustained through a side-slip in conse- quence of excessive road-watering by the Council's workmen. The road had been dry for some days and was the a heavily watered with the result that a mass of mud was formed in which Mr. Turner came to grief. He reported the matter to the C. .T.C. who eventually took the case up, but before it could be heard the Poole Town Council paid the amount claimed with costs. The resolve of the defendants tolcry " Peccavi!" and dispose of the claim made against them without going into tlourt, is in a sense disappointing, for it would have been interesting to have seen the matter fought to a finish. As it is, however, the C.T.C. has succeeded in establishing the principle that Local Authorities may not flood their roads with water in such a way 55 to endanger the life and limbs of any class of road users, aud it is to be hoped that the action of the Club will have far-reaching effects. It should certainly be borne in mind by Warrington cyclists, for many of them have been victims of what seemed exces- sive watering on Warrington Bridge—one of the most dangerous spots for the cyclist in Lancashire and Cheshire. Last summer the bridge, with its net-work of uneven tram-lines, was watered with a persistence that suggested a fear of spontaneous combustion on the part of the authorities.

The announcement that How WE STAND. Mr. Bernard Shaw will deliver an address to-day (Friday) before the London Vegetarian Associa- tion, taking as his text the words, "How We Stand," has given rise to much speculation. When that master hanci decides to expand on "How We All Stand," one would fain understand a subject so grand. Yet, from the title of the forthcoming address, it is difficult to gather how the eccentric , dramatist will treat the theme he has chosen. The answer t "How Do We Stand ? " might well be "On two feet," that is to say, human beings do, but quadrupeds support themselves on four under- standings, and centipedes have no fewer than one hundred. Inquiry into such a subject by such a brain as G. B. S.'s should be most entertain- ing. Again, the answer might well he, "We stand in an erect position, or, as Milton puts it,"Godlike, erect, with native honour clad," instead of having our heads down like the beasts of the fi3ld. This aspect, again, treated by G. 8.5., should be most exalting. As a psycho- logist, he might maintain that while time passes we, during our earthly life,nevertheless in actuality "stand still." Although we think that our ego moves we are mistaken, and a brain like G.B.S.'s could no doubt convincingly demonstrate this hypothesis. Further, if the text "How We Stand " relates to precedence, the answer of course is "We stand in our pro- per order, either as regards seniority or rank." But rank being merely the guinea-stamp, G.B.S. might have a word to say about that. If the ques- tion is to be taken in a Parliamentary sense, and is asked of ,a Tory, the reply would he. "We stand, most of us, unsuccessfully." If the Lord Chamber- lain were asked how we stand, no doubt he would consider that we stand on ceremony. Finally, society stands a lot from some people, especially from G.B.S.

This is the gollen age for " Ham " the new Parliamentarian. SPEECHES. The opening of a fresh House of Commons is always something of ar, opportunity. For the first few weeks the Chamber is certain to be well filled, no matter what the topic under diacussion. The novitiate is making himself at home in his sur- roundings. He is interested in everything, from the:brilliant Minister or ex-Minister and the dash- ing free lance to the acknnowledged bore of whose achievements he has road. When he rises to make his maiden speech, therefore, he is sure of an audi- ence. The present Parliament is exceptional in the matter of the" maiden" speeches that have been crowded into its first days ; but that is a result of the amazing changes in the representation of the country. Several of thoss who have ventured already to address the House have been Labour Members,and generally they have acquitted I themselves with credit. They ara the evidences of the new order of things Parliamentary. But the old order is not entirely extinct. Oae of the new Members to venture on a speech was Viscount Castlereagb,the scion of a house famous in history; and it was interesting to contrast the manner of the young aristocrat with that of the democrats who had preceded him. In both there was modesty ; but to the observer the atmosphere of tradition hung about the phrases of the nobleman, and carried back to the days when maiden speeches were events looked upon with fatherly interest by the elders of the House. Tunes and conditions change, and for the better; but even 80 there is still virtue in continuity.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

_ -- London's Lord Mayor is called upon (as he has just declared in a speech) to sign his name, on an average, 2,000 times a day.

* * The Town Clerk has been granted three months' leave of absence. We hope that the change will result in his being restored to good health. * to, * Will Warrington F.C. win the Northern Union Cup again this year? The question is a very open one,and the answer will be easier after this Saturday's match at Batley.

* * * If any of our readers have tried Oesophagostonaiasis we should like to hear from then. It is a new disease which the Medical Officer df Health haa lately made the acquaintance of. * It is not generally known that the reason why the electorate are only empowered to vote for one candi- date, although two elective auditors have to be elected, is to prevent the office from being captured by " cliques ' or interested parties. Since the compounding scheme was knocked on the head we have not heard much of that old, trusty, rusty servant, the " Water-Lily " at Winwick. A. minute of the Water Committee now shows that the famous beam engine is to be dismantled and repaired. * * * To win a certificate for writing 240 shorthand words a minute is the ambition of Mr. W. G Geohegin of m Drucondra, for whom a special examination is being held in Dublin. lithe examiners have not had special training for pouring out English at this rate they might apply to Alderman Bennett, Warrington. ne can give all the local reporters a start and win easily. * * * Day trips to the Continent are fast becoming popular Last year an excursion was run from Haslingden (Lancs.) to Boulogne, and this year a trip is to be run from the same place to Brussels. Now it is announced that under the auspices of the Nelson Co-operative Society's Education Department a trip to Paris is in contemplation.

* * * Man's canine friend has been making himself dis- tinctly useful this week. The other day a dog in the possession of an Oswaldtwistle collier saved the life of his master's child by barking violently when the infants's clothing caught fire. On Wednesday, in London, a dog saved a whole family from suffocation by whining loudly when poisonous fumes invaded the premises during the night. * * Old-age pensions will cost the nation a lot of money Iwhen they materidise. Mr. Asquith,the Chancellor of the Exchequer, states that the number of persons above 55 years of age enumerated in the census of 1901 was 2,018,716. To provide them with a pension of 58. a week would require over £26,225,000 a year. This should be a mere trifle' however, to a nation which voluntarily spends £187,000,000 a year in drink alone.

* * At last there appears to be a prospect of Warrington having a General Post Office more in keeping with the growing requirements of the town.. Government departments have a way of " hastening slowly " very slowly—and a considerable time has elapsed since it was decided to erect a new office, and the site in Springfield-street was selected. Now, however, tenders have been invited for the carrying out of the work so that the scheme should not be much longer delayed.

* * The workingmen of Warrington appear to realize I their duties towards such a beneficent institution as I the Infirmary, the contributions of the past year hav- ing totalled £960. It is the atiabition of the hard— working committee to raise£l,ooo per annum and their enthusiasm seems to indicate that this worthy ideal will be attained during the coming twelve months. The need for funds is all the greater as the Board of Management have decided upon an extension scheme which has become absolutely necessary and which will involve about £12,000 expenditure.

* The members of the Catholic Young Men's Society must have been rather more relieved than otherwise at being deprived of the opportunity of debating against the taxation of land values with. their Liver- pool rivals. This is one of the questions on which it would be hard to discover a negative argument of any weight, for men of all parties are becoming fully alive to the urgency of this great reform. Moreover, the local debaters thus easily got rid of formidable opponents and obtained two valuable points in the competition.

* 5 * It is becoming very evident that Warrington must have an unenviable reputation with the Local Govern• - me Board—a reputation which we presume has been earned by the long-continued policy of " monkeying." Every application from Warrington appears to undergo very close scrutiny, and oftener than not lately the Board has found means of administering a rebuff. The latest snub for the Corporation is in regard to the new street scheme. Certain expenses incurred thereby were included in an application for power to borrow under the Warrington Corporation Act 1899, but the Board has replied that the Act cannot be regarded as authorising the construction of this street and they have struck out sums amounting to .e,781. The money, we presume, will have to come out of the rates. We hope there will be some reference to this matter at the Council meeting on Tuesday, for it is high time steps were taken to avoid these awkward and not very credit- able disputes with the Local Government Board. *s* We have pleasure in congratulating Messrs. Bolton and England on their return as elective auditors, and at the same time we sympathise with Mr. Bradshaw in ]us narrow defeat. The electors were in the unusual Position of having three good men and true to select from, but out of over 10,000 electors only 1,121 went to the poll. Messrs. England and Bolton were the victors because, though quite independently nominated, they obtained considerable party support. Mr. England had some enthusiastic Liberal and Labour workers, while Mr. Bolton has a good deal to thank Non-trading friends for. The principal point is, however, have we got suitable men for the post? This question may be at once answered in the affirmative. We hope Mr. Bolton will join Mr. England in the undertaking the latter has personally given to explain the Borough accounts at a public meeting held on a year. The innovation is one which would be appreciated in War- rington where economists bulk largely now-a-days.

Notice. The Warrington "Examiner" is now published on Friday afternoon, price one penny, and is ou sale at the various newsagents at about 4 o'clock. Should any difficulty be experienced in getting the paper the proprietors will undertake to deliver a copy at any address in Warrington by 5 o'clock on Friday after- noon without extra charge. Address all communica, tions to" Examiner Office," Bewsey-street Warrington
