RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

CURIOUS RUNCORN PROSECUTION.

At Runcorn, on Monday, Henry Shepherd, licensee of Rutter's Vaults, was fined 20s. and nearly £3 costs for permitting drunkenness. Both licensee and his wife were away at tie time of tie alleged offence. It trans' pired that defendant was frequently at sea, and the Bench instructed the Clerk to call the attention of the licensing justices, as if houses were kept by men who had other employment, and therefore could not look Droperly after the business, it was not fair to other xicensed victuallers nor the public, nor the police, who had to watch these houses in the way they had. A FORGOT TEN LESSON.

At Widnes, on Monday, Terence Dolan, a St. Helens Than, was sent to gaol for three months on a charge of trying to obtain money by means of a trick. On February 20th he wrote to Mr. Ray, an agent for a Wigan colliery, asking him for money because of repre- sentations he had made to his firm respecting the com- pany's coal. In it he stated that he had spoken well to his employers on the quality of their coal, and asked for some allowance in consequence. The police were acquainted with the letter, and it was found that Dolan had never worked at the works in question, and had never been there in his life. The bench said they were determined to put a stop to this practice. Only last Week they had ha.d a similar case before them, and it ought to have been a lesson to him. FALL FROM A TR4IN.—EXTRAORDINARY ESCAPE FROM-DEATH.

I

At a quarter past eleven on Sunday evening, at Widnes, Police-constable Dickenson's attention WWI called to a man shooting for the police in Victoria,•oad, close to the London and North-Western Railway Station. He was apparently under the influence of drink, and on the officer going to his assistance he informed him that he had fallen ont of the 10.45 train from Liverpool to Warrington at Widnes. Examining the man, the policeman found him to be bleeding from the left ear, while the right side of his he id was severely bruised, and his left arra also bore traces of injury. Dr. Brehner was sent far, and ordered his removal to the hospital, where he was detained as an In-patient. When he had sufficiently recovered he gave his name as Thomas Simpson, a seaman, of South Shields, and said be was a passenger by the 1045 to Warrington en route for South Shields. When close to the station, he said, he had fallen out of the train. FALSE PRETENCES AT WIDNES.

I

I

At the Widnes Police Court, on Monday, before the Mayor (Alderman S. Quinn) and Alderman J. W. Ware- ing, Richard Malone (19), of 3, Gas-street, Runcorn,was charged with having obtained the sum of 45.6 d. by false pretences, from Mrs. J. Greenhalgh. Widnes-road. He pleaded guilty.—Superintendent Strickland said that the prisoner had been employed up to the 3rd of February by Mr. Walton of Warrington, who traded as "Naylor's." confectioners. He went round with a van and one of the customers he called upon was Mrs. Greenhalgh. On the 19th February he visited her and said he had had to put the horse up as it had lost some shoes. He asked her for the loan of 4s. 6d. for the purpose of having it re-shod. She gave him the money A day or two afterwards Mr. Walton called and told her he had never authorised the lad to get the money. and he had never known anything of the matter. Prisoner was not in his employ when he borrowed tbe money, and had not been for some time. Prisoner said be had intended to pay the money- back. Fie had bought some food with it. He would never have been the lad he was only for his father having turned him out. Malone, who has a bad record, was remanded for a week in order that inquiries might be made with regard to his parents.

- EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON. MR. SAM WOODS' TESTIMONIAL. ~, The amount received for the testrnonial fund for Mr. ~,• Woods, formerly secretary of the Parliamentary ‘'onamittee of the Trade Unions Congress, and vice- President of the Miners' Federation, now stands at about £lOO. It is intended to close the fund in a few oays' time, but before then Mr. %V. C. Steadman, M.P., hopes that he may have received all outstanding sub- scriptions thatihave been authorised but not sent. MR. J. A. SEDDO'N, M.P., AND SHOP ASSISTANTS' GRIEVANCES. MAIDEN SPEECH IN PARLIAMENT. Mr. J. A. Seddon, M.P.. the Labour member for New- ton I'division, delivered his "maiden "speech in the House of Commons on Tuesday. A motion "that in the opinion of this House it is necessary to put an end , to tines and deductions from wages" was introduced by Sir Charles Dilke. In the course of the discussion Mr. Jr. A. Seddon appealed to the Home Secretary to include the interests of shop assistants in any amendment of the law. The system of "living in," which was a contra- vention of the spirit of the Truck Acts, imposed upon them conditions of life which would not be tolerated by workers of any other class. Such a system was not only economically unfair, but was inimical to the good health and the best interests of those wbo had to submit tokinidt.oflt used. to be said in its favour that it gave a ~.. guardianship to young people who would other- wise be without protection in the perils of city life. However that might once have been, the system had nou degenerated, and had become a mere adjunct to the profits of the shop-keeper. The employer had the right to put before his assistants food which he could not sell on the market, without incurring the penalties of the law; and as for the dormitories, he wished that the Home Secretary would apply to them—for they needed it—the provisions which already regulated common lodging-houses. He was speaking in the interests of something lilm three-quarters of a million of the population, who ought to be thought of in any future legislation on this and kindred subjects. (Cheers.) Mr Herbert Samuel (Under-Secretary to the Home Office) said the Government intended to introduce a measure to end the Truck Act next session. No amendment coul be satisfactory which did not take into account the system of "living in" in shops. Ultimately Sir Charles Dilke's motion was carriod with the addition of the words " °tier than such as are specifically legalised by Parliament."

The Alleged Fradulent Bankruptcy. At the Liverpool County Sessions, en Wednesday, before Mr. W. Scott Barrett and other magistrates, Edmund Green Barratt (28), insurance agent, pleaded not guilty to a charge of having at Warrington in April, 1904, when he vas adjudged bankrupt, failed to discover all his property to the trustee. Mr. Little Prosecuted on behalf of the Official Receiver and Mr. Riley (instructed by Mr. T. S. Steel, Warrington) defended. In opening Mr. Littler said they had to deal with a small bankruptcy, the property in ques- tion being practically all the bankrupt had,or certainly kt.iree-quarters of it. He was accused of failing to 'iscover to the trustee a number of small book debts; A piano, valued at £29; a gold watch, a. sum of £3O tllie to him from his brother, a share under the will of his grandfather, some furniture, and an interest in a 'oaldealing partnership with his brother. All these natters were alleged to have been concealed by the bankrupt, the trustee only hearing of them through iaonymous letters. Evidence in support of counsel's ,':)Pening was given by officials of the Bankruptcy ‘-a.urt. Mr. Riley, for the defence, contended that the Prisoner had not wilfully concealed any items of his Ptoperty,and he had no intention whatever of defraud- aug anyone. No witnesses were called. The jury l'eturned a verdict of " Not guilty," and the prisoner Ivas discharged.

ST. HELENS.

SUDDEN DEATH.

William Seddon (Gi), flint-glass maker, 98, Wilson- street, St. Helens, dropped down dead at fialf-past two, on Wednesday afternoon, near the cemetery gates, St. Helens. It is supposed that he suffered from heart disease.

A SERIOUS OFFENCE.

At the Liverpool Assizes on Monday Edward Melia, 18, glassmaker, admitted having at St. Helens, on December 10, committed a serious offence with a boy. In passing sentence of three years penal servitude Mr. Justice Grantham said it was one of the worst cases ha had ever had to deal with. This concluded the criminal business. ,

ST. HELENS MAN'S DISAPPEARANCE.

The body of William Holme, farmer, of St. Helens, was recovered from the big dam at the Taylor Park about noon on Saturday. Mr. Holme was forty-eight years of age, and lived at a farm close by the park. Some few months ago he lost his wife, and since then has been depressed and in illhealth. He had been missing since Tuesday night, and the various pools in the neighbour- , hood had been dragged by Mr. Byetield, of the Runcorn ' Grappling Corps. The body was found by the St. Helens police, under Sergeant Hudson. The inquest was held on Tuesday at St. Helens Town Hall by Mr. Jones, deputy coroner.—George Millett, of Home Farm, Knowsley-road, St. Helens, step-son, said Mr. Holmes was forty-six years of age. He had been ' depressed since his wife died, last August, and suffered from influenza. On Tuesday last week he drove to St. Helens on business, and came back at two o'clock. At 6.30 he went out and was never seen again alive.—Da id I Wainwright, of 6, Brynn-street, waterman for the Cor- poration, said he found the body in the water on Satur- day, and got it out.—Police-constable Evans said there were no marks of violence.—The jury returned a verdict of "Found drowned," and expressed sympathy with , the relatives.

===== 0002601_19060303_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. HELENS POST OFFICE ROBBERY.

Charles 'Austin Smethurst, a Liverpool youth, who was arrested early on Tuesday morning on his discharge from Walton Gaol, was charged before the St. Helens magistrates later in the day with stealing .£6. 16d. from the sub-post office at the Toll Bar, Prescot-road.—The Chief Constable said that on August 31st last year a bag containing .£5 in gold and Is. 6d. in loose silver was taken out of the drawer at the post office. It was found that prisoner and another lad named Jones went into the shop, and one of them got over the counter and took the money. Jones was arrested, and sentenced to three months' imprisonment. Smethurst, when charged with the offence, said, "I am going to speak the truth, and get it over. Jones put me up to it, but I took the money, and shared it with Jones."—Prisoner was remanded. CORPORATIONS AND TEMPERANCE PLACARDS.

At the meeting of the St. Helens Health Committee on Wednesday the Chairman (Alderman Forster) said he had bad a conversation with the president of the British Women's Temperance Association in regard to the suggestion that the committee should issue placards drawing attention to the evils arising from the abuses of alcohol. It had been pointed out that different towns had treated the subject in different ways. The specimen placard that was submitted to them by the Primitive Methodist Circuit was the same as that issued at Wool- wich, but the Liverpool Corporation had issued a much fairer one, and be suggested that they should follow the example of Liverpool. On the suggestion of Alderman Massy, it was decided to issue handbills as well as placards.

===== 0002601_19060303_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
A GOOD SAVE AT ST. HELENS.

Shortly after six o'clock on Sunday morning Police- constable William Griffiths, of the St. Helens borough force, made a timely discovery of an outbreak of fire in the joiners' shop of Messrs. Ellison and Son, Boundary- road. The officer at once ran to the Town Hall and summoned the fire brigade. The fire had originated in one corner of an extensive joiners' shop, adjoining which was a big sta3k of timber, and underneath there were five horses stabled. Superintendent Lyon and the brigade were quickly on the scene,when the flames were found bursting through one of the windows and break- ing through into the gam mill below. Fanned by the gale that was blowing the outbreak assumed very threatening dimensions, but the brigade were able to deal with it promptly and extinguish the flames. No cause for the fire could be discovered. Great credit is due to Police-constable Griffiths for his promptness, which undoubtedly saved the whole establishment.

ST. HELENS SWIMMING CLUB.

The annual meeting of the St. Helens Club was held on Tuesday, Mr. B. Barr presiding. The report stated that owing to the club not having taken its accustomed position in the League and championships there had been a falling off in the receipts. The Schoolboys' League had been a great success, and St. Thomas's school won the championship for the second time. The membership totalled 106, and Mr. F. W. Gibbons was presented with a medal for obtaining the most new members. Councillor P. Phythian retired from the position of secretary after fifteen years service. Coun- cillor F. R. Dixon-Nuttall was re-elected president. Mr, Jolliffe was elected hon. treasurer ; and Messrs. Slater, Wilkinson, and Kerr were appointed joint secretaries. It was decided that the club should rejoin the N.C.A.S. A. Mr. P. Ripley was elected captain of the first team,with Mr. R. Lomax sub-captain. Mr. C. Pennington was elected captain of the second team. Mr. J. Lawrenso!l was presented with a handsome punch bowl in recogni- tion of his services to the club. WIDOW OBTAINS £l,lOO DAMAGES AGAINST CORPORATION.

Williams v. New St. Helens Tramway Company, Limited, and the St. Helens Corporation.—The hearing of this action, by which Mrs. Isabella Williams, widow of the late Master of the Whiston Workhouse, sought to recover damages for the death of her husband who was killed on the St. Helens and Prescot Tramway by the fall of the trolley and standard, due, it was alleged, either to a defective trolley head belonging to the company, or defective overhead equipment belonging to the Corporation, was concluded at the Nisi Prins Court, Liverpool on Monday.—Harry England, tramway manager, of Wakefield, gave it as his opinion that the car was carelessly or furiously driven round the curve, with the result that the trolley left the wire, and, pro- ceeding in a straight line the boom struck the span wire and the wire became engaged between the trolley head and the wheel. Judging from the result, he thought the speed could not have basis less than ten miles an hour. John M. M'Elroy, manager of the Manchester tramways corroborated the theory of the last witness. Counsel having addressed the jury, and his lordship having summed up, the jury, after twenty minutes' deliberation, found that the accident was caused by the negligence of the Corporation alone. They assessed the damages at £l,lOO, being £2,500 less £1,400 insur- ances.—Judgment was reserved. Mr. Justice Bray gave judgment in the action on Wednesday.—Mr. Kennedy, representing the plaintiff, applied for judgment for £l,lOO, and also for costs on the higher scale, on the ground that the case involved a great deal of scientific evidence.—His Lordship (to Mr. Taylor, K.C., for the Corporation) : You cannot oppose judgment for £l,lOO 2—Mr. Taylor: No, my lord.— His Lordship: I cannot allow costs on the higher scale.—Mr. Taylor : No, there is hothing special in it- -Mr. Sanderson (for the Tramway Company) : I ask for judgment for the Tramway Company.—His Lordship gave judgment in favour of the company, and against the Corporation for £l,lOO with ordinary costs. KILLED IN THE MINE.—EXTRAORDINARY MISHAP.

Mr. Jones, deputy coroner, held the inquest at Hay- dock, on the body of Michael Hannon, aged thirty-two, of Cappagh, co. Galway. who had lodged at 18, Chapel- street, Haydock, and who was killed in the Princess Pit, Haydock,on Thursday night last.—James Hannigan, of 50, Clarence-street, Earlestown,fireman, said the men were working in a road which had to be increased in height. It was well timbered when they, started, but within about ten minutes it fell in and killed deceased. —Joseph Shepherd, of 337, Church-road, Haydock, said they were working with Andrew Melia, a contractor. Deceased was taking some rails up off the road when the roof fell. They thought the place quite safe.— Andrew Melia. of 337 Church-road, Haydock, said that a stone weighing about 28cwt. and some dirt fell out of the roof. It partially covered deceased,and after they had broken the stone they rescued him but he was quite dead.—Mr. Hall, Government inspector' said he could find no definite cause for the accident. The stone had come out of a slip in the roof and cut off the side Just as if the men had done it themselves. It was most extraordinary that it should come down so soon after they started work.—A juryman suggested that deceased might have knocked against a prop and loosened it.—A verdict of " Accidents]. death' was returned.

At Middleton (Lanes) on Saturday Elizabeth Earn- shaw, domestic servant, was charged with the murder of her male child, which, after baying been burned to death, was thrown into the river. The accused, who denied that the child was hers, was remanded.

Hanging to a rafter in a shop at Bourne (Lincolnshire) is a leg of mutton fifty years old. It was bought from the present occupier's predecessor by a gentleman who said he would call for it later on. He never did so, however, and there it hangs to-day. It has so withered that it resembles a shillelagh,

Mr. Edward Clodd has been appointed Chairman of the Rationalist Press Association, Ltd., in succession to the late Mr. G. J. Holyoake.

BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Billiard Match.—Llberal Club v. Conservative Club. An interesting billiard match was played at the Sankey-street Club on Thursday evening when teams representing the Conservative and Liberal Clubs tried conclusions. Twelve game were played and the event resulted in a win for the home men with an aggregate Iscore of 1,720 as compared with 1,590 compiled by the Liberals. The majority for the home team was thus silly 130, a figure which the Liberals are confident of wiping out in the return game at the Liberal Club on Thursday next. Local Bakers' Success. The members of the debating team in connection with this society journeyed to Liverpool on Sunday afternoon to meet the members of the St. Augustine's Literary and Debating Society. The subject for dis- cussion was " The taxation of land values" and the Warrington representatives had been deputed to take the negative. For some unforeseen reason the Liverpool Society were unable to fulfil the engage- ment and in accordance with the rules of the com- petition Warrington claim two points. So far the Warrington debaters have done exceedingly well and figure at the head of the competition.

Mr. T. Wallington. It is a source of great regret tu the congregation of , Wycliffe Church to learn that Mr. Thomas Wallington, one of the Deacons and Church Secretary, has been compelled to relinquish his offices in consequence of the claims of business. Mr. Wallington's work has taken him to Northwich, and he very reluctantly severs his official connection with the Church wherein he has worshipped and worked from his youth upwards. At a Church meeting on Thursday night the explres- sions of regret at Mr. Wallington's resignation were general, and it was decided that a letter of apprecia- tion and thanks be prepared by the Deacons and sent to him.

Warrington Polyglot Society. At the Old Academy, on Wednesday evening, a most interesting paper, read by Mrs. sackson Holmes, was listened to by the members of the German sec- tion. " Erinnerung an Beethoven "—" A Reminis- cence of Beethoven "—was the title. After the paper, a few minutes viva voce practice was the lot, in turn, of each individual present. For next Wednesday night, Mr. H. M. Ashton has undertaken to give before the French Section, some choice selections. From Mr. Cesar Cramer, of Hamburg, who was some time ago a member, the Society has received the gift of a handsome flag of the German nation. The flag was sent to -emphasize expressions of l'entente cordiale between the British nation and his own. Mr. Cramer concludes his letter with three cheers for the Polyglot Society.

, Concert at Messrs. Crosfield's Works. A highly successful benefit concert arranged by the ' members of the Bank Quay Works Sick Club on behalf of J. Unsworth was held in Messrs. Crosfield's ' assembly room on Monday evening. There was a crowdedj attendance. Captain G. It. Crosfield presided and in a brief speech heartily commended the object of the entertainment. Whittle occasioned great merriment. The duties of accom- panists were ably discharged by Messrs. A. E. Trow and T, Taylor.

An interesting wedding took place on Tuesday, when the marriage of Miss Kate Brundrett, daughter of the late Mr. A. W. Brundrett, Dalby Villa, Latch- ford, and Mr. Robert Garnett (Messrs. Wright, Garnet and Wright) son of the late Mr. Robert Garnett of Warrington, was solemnized at Christ Church, Latchford. The officiating clergyman was the Rev. G. C. Briggs. She also wore a handsome sable stole and a hat to match. Her bouquet was of white lilies and orchids presented to her by the Mayor. The bridesmaid I wore a dress of silk voile, and a picture hat. Mr. F. G. Taylor acted as best man. The wedding breakfast was held at Dalby Villa after which the newly married couple left for London and Torquay where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride travelled in a navy blue costume. A large number of hand- some presents were received including a silver card tray from the Warrington Dramatic Society, of which the bride was an active member.

The Examiner, Saturday, March 3, 1906.

Whist Drive at the Academy. An enjoyable whist drive in connection with the Warrington Society was held at the Old Academy on Monday evening. Some 48 players were in attend- ance and the proeedings were highly successful. The first prize in the ladies section was carried off by Mrs. H. M. Ashton whilst Mr. E. S. A. Robson carried off the premier award offered to the gentlemen. Wiredrawers' Dinner. The first annual dinner in connection with the Amalgamated Wiredrawers' Society of Great Britain (Warrington District) was held on Saturday at the Lion Hotel and attracted a large number of wire- drawers' in the district. Favoured with a fine, if somewhat cold evening 243 members assembled round the festive boards in the ballroom. Most of the responsible officials of the society were present, includ- ing the General President Mr. Arnold (Birmingham.) Mr. Lee, the proprietor catered. An excellent menu , was provided, to which ample justice was done. After- wards a very pleasant evening was spent with short speeches and musical items. The Chairman, Mr. W. 'Haigh commenced with a short discourse, saying he . was proud of his position that night, presiding , over such a united body of tradesmen. He was sure every member in the room, together with himself, I was pleased to sae Mr. Arnold. A function like the ' present would not be complete without the General ; President. (Applause.) Every member knew he had helped them through anxious times, but that night he joined them in their pleasures. (Applause.) Such gatherings would certainly tend to strengthen the links which joined them together in brotherhood, and he hoped it would become an annual gathering. (Applause.) The first musical item was a pianoforte solo by Mr. R. G. Haigh, which was brilliantly played. Two glees, " In absence " and " Comrades in arms," rendered by members of the trade under the con- ductorship of Mr. E. Roberts merited the applause accorded them. Mr. Womersley pleased the party with two songs, " My pretty Jane " and " The lost chord." The Chairman called upon the President for a short address. Mr. Arnold thanked the Warrington District for their kind invitation. It gave him great pleasure to meet so large a number of fellow-tradesmen. He expounded the difficulties which beset Trades Union officials, and gave to those present some excel- lent advice for which he was applauded. Mr. Peers Brimelow, flute soloist, earned a well deserved encore for the rendering " The Calif de Bagdad "and " The Bohemian Girl." Mr. L. Wellings gave a carefully written and excellent paper on Trades Unionism, dealing with the early struggles for combination in the 13th and 14th centuries down to the present day. Reminding his hearers of the sufferings and privations ' even Martyrdom by their forefathers for the priviledges they enjoyed to-day and also relating at some length the i present laws governing Trades Unions,showing their position in cam. of a dispute against organised capital since the recent wrecking of the Trades Dispute Bill. Finally putting forward a strong plea for direct Labour Representation in Parliament, as the only 'possible way to regain that which was theirs by right. , Mr. C. Furness supplied the humorous element with ' two recitations in Lancashire dialect " Ab'oth Yeates Speech,"' and "Nomination Day." These were thoroughly enjoyed, provoking much laughter. Messrs. Cook and Clayton very ably sang two duets " The moon hath raised "and "All Well." The audience also received Mr..J. Rowley's musical performance on !glass tumblers with approbation. Mr. N. Cook was applauded for a fine rendering of the song "Anchored," and Mr. A. Forster gained distinction , with the song " Mona," and as an encore sang I" Mary."—The Committee and all present tendered their best thanks to the artistes and all who had con- tributed to the success of the function, including Mr. ,J. Lee, the "host." The singing of the National : Anthem terminated a pleasant and very successful :Igathering.

Wattie Davies, Loos:nom, Will Dayies and Turton : half-backs, Cookson and Oakland; forwards, Settle, Fryer, Fozzard, Jas. Wade, Hodgson, beamont, Tebbutt and Kitson. Reserves, Edwards and Barlow.

Warrington have had two chances of gaining paints since the last issue of the " Examiner, but as both games were away from home success was not looked forward to very confidently. Warrington should, however, have won at Barrow on Saturday. They beat the Furness men with great ease at Warrington earlier in the season and on Saturday they were much the stronger side. But, as on many occasions lately, they failed to score and the match ended in a draw. I have before alluded to the want of effective combina- tion in the back division, and the same fault was in evidence once more. Several times the inside men worked well together only for the chalice to be bungled by the wings. There is no doubt that Fish is badly missed, and if luck had favoured him he should have obtained a lot of points this season, for the forwards are playing better than ever. Most of the credit for what victories have been gained is due to the front-rank men who, either in the pack or in the loose, are a long way above the ordinary. They quite surprised the Barrow folk, who had imagined that their own strength in the scrums would just about gain them the day. If they had taken their chances, Warrington should have won by about a dozen points.

Unfortunately, the weather was wretched and this; ' doubtless, had a lot to do with Warrington's inability to score tries in the approved fashion. Dickinson, after 'an uncertain start, played a very strong game, and 1 with Taylor, was ever in the thick of it. The halves, Hockenhall and Lees were smart also, but the former again spoiled much good w)rk by passing badly at the critical mement. Berry again showed that he is in his rigb, ?lace at full back and did some powerful punting wjaich was of great assistance to the teem. On Tuesday, the weather was worse still for War- rington's appearance at Bradford. There had been snow and sleet all morning, and the ground was not really in fit condition for serious football. It was so sodden that the men could hardly keep their feet, let alone run and pass. Warrington were not quite fully represented, but they put up a strong opposition to the powerful team Bradford had in the field. A couple of goals were recorded against them early on, and then Heseltine got a good try, so that at half-time Warrington were seven points in arrears. They played pluckily through the mud, however, and were' able to keep Bradford at bay for the rest of the game. Once or twice they might have scored, but luck was not with them. Brooks had very hard luck once after followin up, for the ball just went dead as be reached it. The " baby " is not a sprinter, or he would have secured the point. At back, Knight deputised for Berry, and did splendid work under the worst conditions, while the forwards, as usual, were full of go.

I The" A. " team had another hour and a half's I scoring practice on Saturday, when they entertained I Runcorn Fertilisers. The latter created a bit of a I surprise by scoring the first try, but afterwards it was , almost a procession from each scrummage to the line. I The Warrington backs, fed cleverly by Lambert and , Lloyd, accomplished wonders in the way of passing and running, and the total at the close was 8 goals, 14 tries (58 points) to 8 points. There was one matter for regret only, and that was in Ithe A" team's failure to beat record. They only I required either a goal or try, and if Mr. Referee Nevins had not been so strict in the last five minutes, the record of 13 years ago against Rugby would have been eclipsed. The crowd, which was by no means a small one, fairly enjoyed the fun. By now, the "A " iteam's point average has reached colossal proportions.

It was very gratifying to see Isherwood in the field once more, and the popular centre proved that he had lost none of his skill. It is eloquent testimony to the way he makes openings that Jordan on the left wing, made an individual record by scoring seven tries. Some of them were a credit to the scorer as well as his centre. At half back, Lambert again gave a brilliant display,and it is a matter for great satisfaction that this promising junior has at last signed on. He should make a name for himself in the best company before he is a year older.

Warrington "A" are at home again to-clay when the opposition will be furnished by Somerville, a team which has a good reputation in the Manchester and District League. Whether Warrington will get any- where near creating record again is a moot point, but they should win by a substantial margin.

The Stockton Heath Rugby linlon Club is at present going strongly and being only a young combination the members have every reason to feel proud of their record, with a balance in their favour of points scored eight matches have been won and eight lost, the remaining one being drawn. On Saturday they sur- prised the Manchester Y.M.C.A.. team by winning by 21 points to 3. Manchester were confident of victory and the possessors of a heavy pack of forwards they looked to their front rank to obtain the laurel for them. Matters did not however turn out as expected , and though beaten in the scrums the Heathens eight had if anything the best of the arguement. Smith, Cook • and Gibbs were well to the fore. In the back division the two teams were not to be compared the Mancunians being much slower than , the backs of the home team. At half Davenport played well and repeatedly gave the three quarter line chances to shine, chances of which the four fully availed themselves, their combination being excellent. Some exceedingly pretty and effective work was done on the left wing by Ferguson and Bradshaw who between them scored four out of the five tries recorded. At full back W. Gibbs played a sound game and showed that he is developing int-) a really reliable player. It would be well, however, if he tried to avoid the fault of holding to the ball too long.

-- To-day the return match with Heaton Moor is to be played on the Ellesmere-road ground and the Heathens should place another victory to their credit. The team selected is W. Gibbs, full back ; Bouchier, Langley, Ferguson and Bradshaw, threequarter backs ; H. Davenport and Gerrard, half-backs ; Barker,Cook , Daintith, Fawcett, K. Gibhq, Owen, Skelton and Smith, forwards. NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. Rugby Football. [By " Ovallst."]

Warrington's prospects for to-day's important match at Batley do not seem brighter now the selected team is published. 111-luck still dogs the footsteps of the Club, and it is a great misfortune to find at the last minute that the forwards will have to take the field short of two of their heaviest scrummagers in Shugars and Boardman. These two are suffering from injured knees and the regret will he general that they cannot take part in the struggle. If any part of the Warring- ton team has been classed " A 1 " this season, it is the forward division, and the disappointment is keen that the ill-luck of the backs has spread to the front rank. However, luck is bound to turn some day, and if they only get their share on the field of play, the Warrington men should either win or draw.

The selected fifteen will cause considerable surprise in the town, for neither Kenyon nor Widdeson. has caught the eye of the selectors. On the wings will be Taylor and Brooks, two resolute if not very speedy men, while the centres will be the old-tried pair, Isherwood and Dickinson. The one cause for thankfulness is that " Danny "has got himself fit in Itime to take up his old position at left centre. Oh, for Fish! But still, this threequarter line will take a , lot of getting past, and may - I say may—do surpris- ing things on the attack. The half back positions are a Jails bit uncertain, owing to Lees having a rather weak ankle. Should he not be quite fit, Lloyd will partner Ifockenhall. I hope the Oldham man will turn out, however, because he gets the ball away in ranch smarter fashion than any of the reserves. In the rear will be found Berry, who, I am expecting, will distinguish himself greatly. From all accounts he should be superior to his vis-a-vis, whom it would pay Warrington to teat with a little cross-kicking.

The -points of danger on the Batley side are Oak- land at half, and Wattie Davies on the wing. But Warrington know both these men well enough to pay them particular attention. Near the line there is no more dangerous half than Joe Oakland, and the War- rington men will have to be very much alert when the Batley man is within speaking distance of the magic rut.

The Warrington team will be : Berry, fall back; Taylor, Isherwood, Dickinson, and Brooks, three- quarters ; liockenhall and Lees, or Lloyd, half backs; Preston, Belton, Harmer, Thomas, Swift, Jolley Morrison and Heath, forwards.

NOTES FROM BATLEY.

A.Batley correspondent has sent me the following : Batley people are confidently anticipating that their favourites will enter the second round, although they recognise that to defeat Warrington will by no means prove an easy task. Still the "Gallant Youthe " have shown so much improvement since the New Year that it would come as _a great surprise to their partisans if they were to be beaten at home.

-- It will be upon the forwards that Batley will pin their hopes. The pack are in rattling form just now. The veteran Fazzard •is playing like a two-year-old, and amongst his most prominent colleagues ere Hodgson, Settle, and Kitson, three local lads with ' plenty of weight and speed. Dai Fryer, late of Mountain Ash, is another sterling forward and the " wirepullers " will also find Jim Wade a hard nut to crack.

Behind the pack, too, the Batleyites have improved during the last few matches. he Oakland was fiven a rest last Saturday in view of the cup-tie. "Joe has not been playing in his best form latterly, but he may be relied upon to rise to the occasion to-morrow. His colleague Cookson, will be new to the Warrington Spectators. He is very" nippy " and will require a lot of watching. With regard to the threequarters, Batley's! supporters were much encouraged last Satur- day by the brilliant form of their crack left wing ' Wattie Davies. Should he reproduce this form on , Saturday more than one man will be required to watch him. Frank Loosernore will be his centre. He is , another Welshman and has done splendid service for Batley this season. Will Davies is hardly as reliable 'as his namesake, but his great speed makes him a dangerous man to tackle. On the right wing Turton will figure. His diminutive proportions are no criterion ! as to his ability, as the man deputed to mark him will ' find out.

-- The Batley Pustodian, White, formerly acted in the same capacity for Castleford. Ile has shown some- what in and out form, and is rather slow in fielding the ball, but he is a fine kick, using either foot with equal facility.

The committee met on Thursday night and selected the following team :—Back, White ; three-quarters,

MR. A. H. CROSFIELD'S PARLIAMENTARY IMPRESSIONS.

Interesting Speech on the Work of the

Government.

MR. W. MADDEN'S LECTURE ON "DANIEL O'CONNELL."

A large audience assembled in the Parr Hall on Thursday evening, when Mr. W. Madden delivered a highly interesting lecture on " Daniel O'Connell : what he did for civil and religious liberty." The meeting was presided over by Mr. A. H. Crosfieid, M.P., who spoke in a happy style of his Parliamentary impressions, and alluded to some of the great ques- tions which will occupy the attention of the Govern- ment. .

Mr. CROSFIELD, M.P., who was enthusiastically received, said that as that was the first occasion since a rather memorable day—(applause)—that he had had the pleasure and privilege of addressing his fellow- townsmen, friends of his had suggested that he ought to give them just a few experiences in that new sphere of life upon which thanks to their kindness and to the splendid and energetic work of many of those who were present that night —(applause)—under the spirited and splendid and brilliant leadership of Mr. Madden— (applause)—he had entered. They suggested that he should say a word or two about politics and his diffi- culty was that he knew some Conservative friends were present—(langhter)—and they knew he was a sad politi- cian—(laughter)—and so he could only throw himself 1 upon their indulgence and ask them to forgive any of those political indiscretions to Waich party politicians I were unfortunately addicted. (Laughter.) He took it that those of them who were Progressives might be forgiven if, metaphorically speaking, they exchanged a hand-shake after

===== 0002601_19060303_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GREAT VICTORY

to which he had alluded. (Applause). He was sure that anything he might say about his political opponents I would be said with no unkindly feeling because, though he had at any rate inherited, he thought,frorn his father a certain faculty of hitting out straight—(applause)— when politics were in the wind, he hoped he had also inherited from him an entire lack of animosity towards those who honestly differed from one in politics. (Hear, hear). He wanted to say a word just to cheer up their friends who, from what he read in the papers, were evidently at the Junior Conservative Club a little bit cast down. (Laughter). He did not want to dwell too much upon that, but they seemed to be rather publicla hanging out signals of distress and there appeared to be some woe and. lamentation in those quarters. (Laughter). He advised their friends to have a little patience and he thought when they had considered matters they would come to the conclusion—which possibly a good many of them in their hearts might already have , arrived at—that it was no more a bad thing for them than it was for the Progressives to have had a change of men and a change in the political regime. (Applause.) Whatever their political complexion might be, or what- ever might be their religions or other views, it would no more have been a good thing for their friends at the Junior Conservative Club than for any part of the con- stituency to deal a heavy blow at the great trade of Lancashire and at the great shipping trade of this country—a blow which could not fail to have been felt in our own iron and leather manufactories and iu many other branches of industry. Although Free Trade was the great Issue, he ventured during the contest they recently had in Warrington to emphasise the fact that they did not and never would as members of the Pro- gressive party light a merely negative battle, and he could assure them that every minute he had spent in that august assembly—which he was going to say was at the centre of civilization,only it just flashed across his mind that his father would never tolerate that expres- sion as applied to London because he always said that if it were not for Lancashire and Yorkshire London would go spark out—(laughter and apnlause)— every minute spent in the House of Commons, although one was not in a position to speak relatively and compare it with previous gather- inks at Westminster, had impressed him with the fact theta negative policy was the very last thing that that assembly had gathered together to carry out. (Aplause.) A more TITO/LOUGH AND EA:IISM

body of men he had never come across. Their friends must forgive him if he particularised a little ; he was speaking of one particular quarter—a very large quarter —of the House. (Laughter.) As regarded another quarter he wanted to be quite charitable and kind, but at the present moment,tbough be had not seen anything ill-natured, he must say the Opposition were very like a laughing stock in the House. (Laughter.) It was unfortunate that some of their ablest men had not been returned and there was universal regret that, for instance, men like Lord Hugh Cecil and Mr.„Gibson Bowles—(applause)—and others were missing on that side. He (Mr. Crosfield) would not give that meeting any description of it himself, but he would tell thorn what was said by one of those witty fellow-countrymen of theirs, who still, he perceived, had a knack of hitting a nail on the head more rapidly than any other people in the House. (Applause.) There wis a speech delivered the other night by Mr. Houston, who represented a division in Liverpool, and he (Mr. Crosfleld) had no doubt the speaker intended to amuse the House and he certainly succeeded in doing so. It was questionable whether Mr. Houston's speech alto- gether pleased some on his own side sitting below him on the lint and second Conservative benches, because, after all, everybody recognized that it was an excellent piece of buffoonery and nothing else. Mr. Houston went ahead at full steam and was received with uproarious laughter on the Liberal side, and after about a quarter of an hour he paused. Then there came a voice, he believed from one of the Irish benches : " Will said the Conservative party hadn't got a leader?" (Loud laughter.) He (Mr. Crosfield) could tell them a lot, speaking from a personal point of view, but he wanted for a moment to refer not to men but to measures. He thought great satisfaction would be felt in Warrington, as it would be felt throughout the whole country, at the courageous and, he maintained, thoroughly just manner in which the Government had dealt with the very difficult and, if they liked, even to some extent dangerous problem which had been left for them to deal with in

SOUTH A Fate A. (Hear, hear). He was referring equally on the one hand to the new Constitution which they were going to introduce and on the other to the firm and courageous measures they were taking to deal with the ill-ornend introduction of what during the election he called quasi-slavery and semi-slaverv—eKpressions which he did not feel the slightest need to qualify or withdraw in any shape or form. (Applause). In that connection he might say that amongst the many important speeches that had been made since the opening of Parliament, he thought the weightiest and most important was the one delivered by the new Lord Chancellor, Lord Lorebnrn, who wa% better known to a good many in the old days as Sir Robert Reid and who never hesitated to face the music in the dark days of the South African imbroglio. His rebuke to Lord Miler he (Ur. Crosfleld) thought was most thoroughly well merited. As another indica- tion of the intention to deal seriously, earnestly and thoroughly with all the problems that came before the Government, he might mention the way in which the Liberal benches were filled the other night when Sir Charles Wilke was discussing the question of fines—not so much as applied in great industries where trade unions could defend themselves, though that was to be considered too, hut particularly the exceslive and

, CRUEL SYSTEM OR FINES in some of those industries which were not protected by great trade unions, such as the shop assistants and the seamstresses who did their work at home. They found the House listening to every detail during the discussion Sir Charles raised on that point, showing as he always did the marvellously full knowledge of every subject which he tackled. He (Mr. Crosfleld) had also the pleasure of listening to a speech from a man who along with Sir Charles—he alluded to Professor Bryce , —was considered perhaps better informed than anyone else in the whole House of Commons. The House listened with attention and respect to members whose great information, as in these cases, commanded it, but still it was the man who had the magnetic quality and personality—and of course, the speakers he had referred to possessed a striking parsonality--who really commanded the closest attention and greatest sympathy of the House. He could assure them that when a representative! like Mr. Crooks got up he was received with the sympathy and interest that men of greater intellectual attainments than his could not co.nmand. (Applause.) One of the most striking and to him (Mr. Crosfield) almost touching incidents that occurred was the maiden speech in office of that plucky and splendid Parliamentarian. Mr. John Burns. (Applause.) He used the word "plucky " advisedly because he thought it wanted some courage for a man like that not to be afraid of any jeers that might be raised because, out of respect to the King, he chose to put on the uniform that wascustomary when attending one of His Majesty's receptions and because he came to the Premier's recep- tion bedizened with his insignia, if they liked, which was borne in a manly way. He had earned his position and he knew full well that he was giving the country full value for it. (Applause.) Mr. Burns was not only plucky but full of humour and thorough to a degree. In dealing with the complex q uestion of Tsp; UNEMPLOYED which, as he truly said, was so complex and needed so many different remedies just as it arose from so many and diverse unfortunate causes,'Mr. Burns showed a true and statesmanlike judgment and one could not help think- ing that in the whole history of humanity it was perhaps an unexampled position that a man born a working man should be standing there in the greatest Parliament that the world had ever known, holding the Rouse for an hour when not a man went out and on both sides every- body listened with respect to the well-informed speech

and the vigorous and courageous action described in that speech by Mr. John Barns. Continning,Mr. Cros- field referred to the great impression created in his mind by the leader of the Irish party in the Elouse.(Applause.) There were so many qualities that were so apparent the moment one saw and listened to

MR. REDMOV3 (Applause.) There was first of all plainly determination and at the same time perfect good humour. There was sagacity, and in addition to those qualities there was a genuine eloquence when Mr. Redmond was speaking, and he was one of those men who had the gift of hold- ing the House. He certainly did hold the House in the strong speech he made in favour of Irish autonomy during the debate on the King's address. During the election in Warrington he (Mr. Crosfield) tried to be as frank as he could, and he wrote a letter to the papers explaining that, acting as they were in alliance with their Free Trade Unionist friends, it would mit be fair to use their votes to try and carry a Bill for the creation of a Legislative Assembly in Dublin with an executive responsible to it. He thought it only right to make that position clear, but they knew that ever since that question first came to the front he had felt that just as Liberal policy had strengthened the Empire from end to end of the world, so in Ireland

AN IDEAL POLICY was to create an Assembly like that and throw real responsibility upon the people. In the meantime the work of this Government—it carrying out that policy, be granted, but doing as be knew they intended to do in associating the Irish people with their own Govern- ment and making the Government in Ireland according to Irish ideas leading up. as Mr. Haldane said, to the Government of the people, by the people, and for the people "—was the very best way to strengthen the empire and to do justice in Ireland. (Applause). He also believed they were animated by like principles in carrying out the Lord Chancellor's policy in that country which had been the scene of to much suffering and disaster—South Africa. Going back twenty years one remembered the blow to Progressives in England when a liberal and broad- minded Irish polidy was advocated in his old age by a man of great courage and energy who, along with Mr. Parnell, strove to turn back a stream of marked racial hatred which for so many centuries had bred

NOTHING RUT MISCHIEF not alone to Ireland but just as much to this country. I They could not help remembering that in the twenty years that had elapsed already the most unpopular part of that policy—the great land purchase scheme—had in effect been carried by the very people who denounced it. They could not forget that the trust they put in the Irish people in carrying a measure of that kind but ill- consorted with the previous distrust which bad so long made them apparently unwilling to give the Irish people what Sir Redvers Buller called the other day "a little generous treatment." (Applause.) But the strides had been very great and the whole feeling towards Ireland was different now. He (Mr. Crosfleld) had told them before of the speech that Mr. Redmond delivered when through the jeers and interruptions by those in the House of Commons he (Mr. Crosfield) did not hear one syllable from beginning to end. Better feelings were now prevailing and, looking back to one's earlier experience, one could not forget the efforts made by men in those days which were now bearing fruit. When they had listened to Mr. Madden's lecture he knew they would feel that, reflecting on the life of the great Irish liberator whose magnetic personality gave him 'unprecedented influence over the whole Irish nation,one, I realized that he too had sown good seed which in the future would bear fruit for the benefit of his beloved country. (Loud applause.) Mr. W. MADDEN then proceeded with his lecture which was a most eloquent and instructive one. Daniel O'Connell, he said, was a great politician, a great states- man, a great Irishman and a great Catholic. (Applause.) Gladstone said he was the greatest leader of men that he had ever known. Thackeray said O'Connell might be a great humbug taut he was certainly a great man,and in the biographical sketches which appeared in the London papers it.was stated that O'Connell was truly the leader of a people and not the director of a party. (Applause.) The time when O'Connell was born was only removed by about 100 years from the darkest history not only of the Irish people but of all the civilized nations of Europe. Freedom, as we under- stood it, either religious or civil, did not exist. In Ireland they had an aristocracy and nobility with a certain amount of political power, whilst the great mass of the people had neither political power nor the right to exercise their religion. No Catholic could worship God as he pleased or marry as he pleased. Everything was done to destroy the feeling of freedom or the growth in power of the peasantry and the same thing was done in a modified way with regardto the Nonconformists of England. A bond of union existed between English Nonconformists and the Irish Catholics in their troubles. Mr. Madden then traced the career of O'Connell in a most interest- ing way and described his great efforts on behalf of Irish liberty. His was the first brain that conceived the idea of fighting the Government, which had filched away the rights of the Irish, by the uprising of the people in a constitutional manner. O'Connell's gnat struggle for Catholic Emancipation was alluded to, and Mr. Madden pointed out that when Peel and Wellington, who had fought against emancipa- tion for many years, saw that the Irish were on the brink of rebelion, that which they would not concede out of a spirit of justice was conceded out of a spirit of fear. O'Connell obtained 800,000 Catholic signatures in Ireland in support of Lord John Russell's motion to repeal the Tests and Corporation Acts which were directed against English Nonconformists. When returned to Parliament O'Connell had the courage to refuse to take what to him was a blasphemous oath. It was he and his fifty three faithful Irish supporters who carried the Reform Bill which freed the English people. Not only was O'Connell the friend of his own people but the friend of all mankind. He was a supporter of Cobden and Bright and helped them in their assertion of Free Trade ; and he was an uncom- promising opponent of slavery. He proved that it was possible to have liberty with order and independence with loyalty, to be a friend of one's fellow-men without being an enemy of the King. Mr. Madden closed the lecture with a tribute to O'Connell's deeply religious character.

- -The lOC-wee was well illustrated with lime-light views. The Rev. Father Wrr,soN moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Madden. He said that as the audience had fol- lowed the wonderful story of modern liberty so marvellon,ly illustrated by the career of him who seemed to have been the very source of modern European liberty, it appeared to them that Mr. Madden must have drunk of the same sacred fountain of eloquence as Daniel O'Connell himself. (Applause.) Mr. McAvov seconded.

I Mr. CROSFIELD, M.P.. in supporting said they had had the pleasure of listening to perhaps the most eloquent lecture ever delivered in Warrington. (Ap- planse.) It had given them a review of the life of one of the greatest statesmen the United Kingdom could look upon with pride and whom it delighted to honour and at the smile time ar. Madden had given them a splendid impression of the devotion to country and to a beautiful religious belief felt by the great liberator of Ireland as it was evidently felt by the lecturer himself. (Applause.). . .

The motion was ent uslas icily carried. Mr. 0. TOOLE moved a vote of thanks to the chairman and pointed out that in supporting the Liberal party in the recent division on the Irish question Mr. Crosfield had acted in keeping with the Parliamentary career of O'Connell. (Applause). Mr. M. O. IigARNE seconded and -the vote was cordially passed. In responding Mr. CROSFIELD said hisfinterest in the Irish question was not taken up because there was an election in the wind. For over twenty years he had followed the question carefully and he had stood steadily by the cause o giving the Irish responsibility for their own affairs. He had looked forward to better things for the sister Isle under those happier conditions and he had been as thankful as any Irishman in that room to see the progress that had been made. It had given him extreme prid3 and pleasure to'preside over a meeting addressed by a gentleman who had done the Liberal party not to mention himself (Mr. Crosfield) such inestimable service in the great battle they had to fight. (Applause). The recent election had brought Catholics and Protestants together in a spirit of proper toleration. (Applause.) If old Dr. Johnson was cor- rect in saying that" moderation" was the finest word in the English language, he ( \fr. Crosfield) ventured to think the cordial spirit of toleration was not a bad second to it. (Applause.) During the evening an interesting musical programme was submitted, including songs by Miss A. Richardson, and Miss P. M. Rigby ; duets by Messrs. P. and J. Bate, and choruses by thelchoir of St. Mary's, Buttermarket- street. Mr. J. NV. McAvoy accompanied, and Mr. John Eckersley conducted.

Warrington Sailing Club.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Sailing Club I was held on Monday evening last at the Railway Tavern, Bank Quay, Mr. 11. Warburton presiding over a good attendance of members. The election of officers for the coming season resulted as follows : Mr. 11. Warburton, comodore ; Mr. J. Deverson, vice- comodore ; Mr. IV. Warburton, jun., hon. sec.; Mr. J. . Looker, hon. treasurer ; committee, Messrs. W. Warburton, sen., C. Packer, J. Smith, H. Sherratt, T. Whitfield and J. Taylor. Three races were arranged for the season, the first to take place on the Saturday after Good Friday, April 11th. After the business was over an enjoyable smoker was held.

CRICKETERS' DIARY.

Messrs. Bussey's wonderful little waistcoat pocket ,cricketer's diary and companion has again been issued. The amount of- information between its neat covers is surprising, and the cricket enthusiast, whether a player or merely an "arm-chair critic " could not invest 6d. in anything better. In addition to the memorandum pages; the printed pages give the results of County, University and leading school matches ; of the Gentle- men v. Players ; and the matches with visiting teams ; the relative positions of counties; the aggregate scores made by and against visiting teams ; the batting aver- ages of the year ; the bowling averages of the year ; the high scorers for the past season ; results of matches —England v. Australia, both at home and in the Colonies ; results of Australian Inter-Colonial matches records of interest. The diary also gives the principal fixtures for the coming season ; the latest revised laws of cricket; Tit-bits of useful information to cricketers.

John King. one of the oldest drivers in the service of the Great Eastern Railway, who was fireman of the train by which the King and Queen travelled to Sand- ringham on the occasion of their honeymoon in PM, has just died at Norwich.

ALL TO PIECES.

"All to pieces." This is a form of complaint we often hear of—the system out of condition, temper uncertain, so that the slightest irritation gets on the nerves, rest- less. no appetite,no sleep that is refreshing. Now what is wanted is something to pull you together. Get Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, and you will soon , find that it will pull you together and ! that you will be all right again. Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters will do you good, for it contains every- thing necessary for fighting wasting disease. There is 'no doubt about it. Only taken in time, it will ward off Early serious illnesses. Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters strengthens that part of the system which is weakest or has been weakened by disease, and therefore more liable to coils and their attendant ailments. At Warrington on Saturday Berthelot Czarpinski was charged with stealing a pair of clippers and a razor, valued Ils.,the property of William IVlailing,hairdresser, Liverpool-road.—Prosecutor was in the kitchen when he heard a noise in the shop, and on going there saw the prisoner leaving by the front door. MAKING GRAPHOPHONE RECORDS.

Everyone interested in Talking'Machines is familiar with the term " gold moulded records," but not every- one understands exactly what is meant. Sometimes it even occurs that attempts are made to deceive the public by such statements as "our records are gnaran- teed to contain as much gold as those of any other make." There is of course, no gold in the composition used in making the record cylinders. But the best records are cast in moulds which are lined with pure gold,q hence the expression gold moulded." Unless a record cylinder is produced from a gold mould, it is harsh in tone and wears badly. Every Columbia Cylinder Record is a genuine gold-moulded ecord, and " wears like iron." Colombia Records are also made for disc graplaophones and gramophones. A complete list, together with the March supplement will be sent free to all who write for" Price Book 20 " to the Columbia Phonograph Co., General, 89, Great Eastern Street, London, E.C. FISH LIVING IN HOT WATER.

A Mexican naturalist claims to have discovered a species of fish which lives and flourishes in a hot spring in a remote part of Colorado. How these creatures manage to exist in water almost hot enough to boil them is a mystery to be solved by scientists, who are now said to be investi- gating the wonder. The fish are described as being of a light red colour, about three inches long; they are shaped sonievrhat like a pike, with large mouths and bright, prominent eyes. They move very slowly, having none of the swiftness and activity of ordinary fishes, which is perhaps hardly to be wondered at. The Mexican had great difficulty in securing speci- mens. Rook and line were useless. as the fish were indifferent to bait, however tempting. Finally a a small net was procured and two of the fish captured. The spring is well known to the Indians of the loonlity, who regard its waters as curative, and hold the fish in superstitious regard, believing that from them the water derives its healing qualities.

BB BRAVE ANI) TRUE.

A young girl was deeply concerned about her spiritual interests, and after a severe struggle, started to visit her parlor to ask him to show her the way of As she entered an omnibus in carrying out her pui pose, she saw seated there several of her friends, who asked her where she was going. The tempter immediately said to her: "Don't tell the is where you are going; but answer in eameevaeive wey." At the same time the Spirit whispered to her: Be brave and conscientious about this. Tell them of your purpose, and ask them to go with you. She obeyed the latter voice. Her friends declined to accompany her, and she went on alone. When she came to the minister's house, he came to the door to meet her. She paused from embarrassment for an instant, and then said:

Doctor, I started to come to see you to ask you to lead me to Christ; but now I am here, I have come to tell you that I have found Christ." As she went, she was cleansed.

===== 0002601_19060303_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
BOYS WHO CANNOT.

"It is a peculiar but undoubted fact that there are a few mere ordinary things that, in some par- ticular cases, boys are seemingly quite incapable of performing," said a genial head master the other day to a writer in Chums. "Really, though," he added hastily, " I ought not to admit this, for I may, perhaps, simply be inviting some lad to come and plead that he 'really can't do his lessons.' More than once I have come across boys who, even if you offered them the reward of a new cricket bat, could not, tie a double bow in the orthodox manner. One youth—he is now a man—to this day ties his double bow by making a single bow, pushing the long end of the string through the loop just formed, and then drawing tight in the usual way. Not a few boys make a terrible bungle of tying their neck- ties, and I have even had parents write to me and specially ask that I would have their sons taught better, for ' Charles always looks so very untidy about the neck.' One of my assistants on a certain occasion stopped a fight between two pupils, and discovered that the cause of complaint of one of them was that the other would persist in ruffling his hair. Now, this was taken to be quite an excellent joke, for the lad in question had a head that usually appeared as if it had just been dragged through a thorn bush. The fact was, however, that the one trouble of this young gentleman's existence was that, try as he would, he could not make an even parting in his hair. A boy sleeping in the same dormitory, it was learned, kindly came to his assistance each morning, and drew a straight line with the comb for him. The parting thus formed, though, had to last all day, and other boys, knowing this, took good care that it did not. With a penknife and a piece of wood some boys will carve you almost anything. Others can never even sharpen a pencil. We had one boy who drew atrociously, but whose dearest wish •ou away for a was to become an artist, lie might as well have aimed at being Emperor of China. Cut a pencil he while, scould.naw would would whittle lead,dwhittlearid he would start all clumsily over again."

===== 0002601_19060303_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
CALL RIGHTS RFSERTED] DO WE EAT TOO MITCH ?

===== 0002601_19060303_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
DR. ANDREW WILSON (Author of "A Manuel of Health Science," ifc.).

The discussion which periodically takes place on this subject, as is naturally to be expected, evokes a large amount of public interest. We are all more or leas directly concerned with the important function of nourishing our bodies, seeing that to nourish ourselves is the first duty we perform on entering this world, and the last duty we discharge on leaving it. The subject of the amount of the food required is, of course, a strictly scientific one—ill other wcrds we can only, arrive at definite conclusions in the matter through an appeal to scientific knowledge con. .?erning the quantity of food required under dif- ferent circumstances of life. The science of foods and feeding has of late years been very considerably advanced through researches made not merely into the composition of foods and the duties they discharge in our living economy, but also concerning the amount of food required to produce a given amount of " energy " or working power. It should not be forgotten, in the first place, that the functions of food in the body are of a two-fold character. If we com- pare the body to a locomotive engine we find an accurate parallel between that complex piece of machinery and the still more complicated, organism we ourselves own. As the structural material of the engine differs very materially from its energy-producing fuel, so it may be said the foods upon which we subsist exhibit a like difference, even if it is one of not so rigid a character as regards tissue-forming foods and energy-producing articles of diet. The line be- tween these two classes of foods is not a sharply defined one seeing that the two classes of foods may, under certain bodily conditions, somewhat overlap as regards their functions. Thus the body-building foods may, and probably do, contribute somewhat to the development of "energy," whilst the energy producing foods are regarded by many physiologists as capable of giving origin to substances which may be employed in the building up of our tissues. All the same, the division just alluded to is a thoroughly scientific one, and in the main may be regarded as separating the articles on which we subsist into two clearly defined classes or divisions.

BODY-BUILDERS AND ENERGY-PRODUCERS.

The first class of foods—those which go to build up our bodies—are termed "nitrogenous foods, for the reason that amongst the chemical elements of which they are composed nitrogen forms a conspicuous item. Such foods are re- presented by white of egg, juice of meat, curd of milk, gluten of flour, legumin of peas, beans and lentils, and like substances. These are typically our body building substances. They go to form the basis of our living tissues, and therefore correspond in a very direct sense to the material of which the engine is composed. The second class includes " non-nitrogenous" foods. These are represented by fats, starches and sugars. It may here be mentioned that all the starch we consume (in the shape of bread, potatoes, tapioca, rice, &c.) requires to be changed in the progress of digestion into sugar before it can be utilised by the body. Hence starches and sugars in respect of our nutrition mean practically the same thing. They repre- sent our energy producers. There can be little doubt that out of the starches and sugars we consume—and of this material a considerable amount is taken each day—we develop the power of doing muscular work. Fat is also an energy-producing food. Its chief function, however, is the development of heat, but as we may regard the production of heat as being synonymous with the development of energy, we may include fats, starches and sugars to- gether as the foods out of which and from which is developed our working power. In addition to these foods we demand a large quantity of water each day, this fluid being necessary for the digestion of other foods, and because being perpetually parted with by lungs, skin and kidneys, it requires replacement as item an essential in the composition of our living matter. The human body indeed consists by weight of two-thirds of water. Minerals also form an important part of our diet. They discharge certain important func- tions in connection with the digestion of foods, and are required in order to promote the due and proper passing of the blood. An excellent example of the necessity for minerals foods is found in. the fact that we require phosphate of lime when growing, in order to form our bones. Similarly we place common salt on our tables because that mineral is a necessity for the per- fection of the gastric juice of the stomach, while it also discharges other functions in the body. The absence of salts of potash from the food causes that disease formerly only too well-known amongst sailors, namely, " scurvy."

THE REGULATIONS OF QUANVITY.

Turning now to the amounts of food which are required under different conditions of life, we find that the quality as well as the quantity of food necessarily varies according to age, while sex plays an important part in the deter- mination of the amount of food required. Occupation is also another condition which has to be taken into account in coanection with not merely the quantity, but also the quality of the food consumed. Hence no rigid rule Can be drawn with regard to the quantity of food con- sumed unless circumstances of the individual life are taken into account. It is the omission of this latter feature which renders unscientific discussions regarding the amount of food re- quired for our healthy support somewhat value- less. An excellent example of the regulation of the quantity of food consumed and of the selec- tion of a particular quality with reference to the work a human body has to perform is found in the case of prison dietaries. The scale of feeding naturally varies according to the labour the prisoner Ls called upon to perform. The ap- plication of this principle to ordinary life can be readily estimated, seeing that two men living utterly different lives require not tuerel a diet of a different quality, but likewise require their food to be supplied in different amoants: A comparison of the food required per day for the support of a man doing ordinary work reveals certain interesting features. If we take the tnean or average calculations derived from food tables compiled by three of the most distin- guished physiologists who have investigated this question, and m!tking no allowance for water either taken as water or for that contained chemically in the food, and likewise neglecting the question of any waste, we find the average amounts required by the man per day to work out as follows :—Nitrogenous food 431 ounces, I fats 353 ounces, starches and sugars 11.71 ounces, and minerals 1.00 ounce. The total water-free food per day thus amounts to 2055. ounces.

lithe man is put on the other band to very hard work and the diet calculated under the same circumstances—that is to say the amount of nutritive material which ii,must be placed absolutely at the service of his body—it would represent a total of from 26.7 to 31 ounces. Nitrogenous foods would be given to the extent of 6 to 7 ounces, fats from 3i to 4?)- ounces, starches and sugars from 16 to 18 ounces, and the minerals from to 11 ounce. Here we find illustrated the genral principle that where the human engine is called upon to perform a greater amount of work we must stoke, it _accordingly. Ilavint; thus obtained a

===== 0002601_19060303_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
'ch 3, 1906.

acanuarit or average tor ormnary and hard wont, it is obvious that the answer to the question " do.we eat too much ?" will depend materially upon the circumstances of the individual life.

===== 0002601_19060303_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
CIRCVMSTANCRS ALTER CASES.

The sedentary man does not require the same amount of food as the hewer of wood and drawer of water working hard with his musoles, but what is of vastly greater 'importance is the recognition of the fact that the brain worker and sedentary liver requires his food presented to him in a shape much more readily adapted for easy digestion than the man who may be deemibed as the toiler turd moiler. Your country ploughman may make a very hearty and sub- stantial meal off bread and cheese, a form of food which both as regards its i.inality and its quantity would be utteTly indigestible by 1111 city man. We see that the question of feeding therefore does not resolve itself no Mach into it question of quantity as really into a question of quality of food, but that condition, which cer- tainly operates most definitely in so far as the question of over-eating is concerned is that re- presented by the question of age. A wise physician dealing with the question of age and with the occurrence of extreme stoutness or corpulence remarks that many persons neglect the important physiological rule that the need of food naturally diminishes with advancing years, just as the capacities for digesting and assimilating and duly utilising food diminish also. He adds that "It is difficult to make a man of forty understand that he cannot pro- perly utilise and adequately dispose of as much food as he used to when thirty, or a man of fifty as much as when he was forty ; so that a diet which would not be in anyway excessive at the former age, becomes distinctly excessive and productive of undesirable corpulence on the liver."

DIFFERENT MRS DTFFERENT MEALS.

It is of importance, therefore, to recognise the great law that with advancing age the quantity of food required cUrninishes simply because the working power of the body itself decree see, and the human engine therefore requires less stok- ing. We may also note that a wise selection of foods forms another important item in connec- tion with the question whether or not we eat too much. For example, if a person takes each day only half an ounce of sugar over the amount which is necessary for him, and this sugar be stored up in the body as fat, we should find him to represent an increase of weight per annum of over eleven pounds, amounting in five years .to four stones of increase. Here again we find illustrated the important principle that the quality of food should be regulated by the Cir- cumstances of the individual life. Jast as the thin, spare and lean person may take a fair allowance of starch and sugar, and especially fat as a heat forming food, so the person who is inclined to make flesh, so to speak, on little food will require to adopt the opposite regime, and a spare dietary in the latter case will be found to be that which is most productive of health. After all it is the wise individual regula- tion of life connected, with foods and drinks which alone can guide us in the pathways of health. Each person is a law unto himself or herself, not merely in the matter of foods and drinks, but even of the drugs by aid of which the physician treats our diseases. It is undeniable that the tendency of our age lies in the direction of luxury, over-feeding appearing naturally as part and parcel of the luxurious mode of living in which too many of us indulge. Those who have experienced the increased vigour both of body and mind which results from the adoption of a dietary which is just sufficient for the due development of their bodily and mental work and no more, will readdy. testify to the fact that the man who over-eats loses much of the rational enjoyment of life.

LOCAL SHEPHERDY.

A Successful Year in the Warrington

District.

The annual report of the Warrington district of the Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds has just been issued and contains the following interesting information On the threshold of the year 1906, it affords us great pleasure to submit to you the annual statement of accounts of both the District and Lodges for the year 1905, and in doings() we trust that the same will receive at your hands the thoughtful attention they deserve. It will be perceived on reviewing the work for the past twelve months, that 47 new members were admitted, while during the same period we lost through death 10, and 36 seceded from the District, leaving the member- ship at 904, or an increase of one over the previous year. Turning to the financial side, the fends of the District stand at £MI 12s. 91d., or an increase in round figures of £4O, while the accumulated capital of thenine Lodges nowamounts to £5330 2s. 01d. I as against £5,204 19s. 5d., or an actual gain of £125 2s. 71d. The Lodges have during the year paid to its sick members the sum of £705 ss. 111 d. as against £773 7s. the year previous. The funeral claims amounted to £l9oor a decrease of £3B from 1904. It will be noticed that several Lodges have had an unusual amount of sickness amongst their members. This is unfortunate, both from a Lodge's and a sick brother's point of view, but there is consolation and satisfaction in knowing that the sum so distributed must have been in many caae.s a source of benefit and comfort, and that the fore- thought and good management in safeguarding and in- vesting in highclass securities, the surplus income in favourable years has not been in vain and without its effect. It is, however, pleasant for us to reflect that on the whole the past year has been satisfactory. We have every cause to be thankful that we are in our present position, especially when we consider that the great depression which prevailed during the year 1904 still continued for the better portion of the past year. There are signs that the prospect for the future,As regards employment and trade generally, is much brighter and that the dark cloud of depression is slowly but surely lifting: We are hoping and trusting that such signs in the revival of trade will not only benefit the working classes, but that our par- ticular Order and District will have a fair share of such prosperity. We should be remiss in our duty if we allowed the opportunity to pass without placing on record our appreciation and thanks to those brethren who, year in and year out, have by their effort; assisted in promoting the true welfare and success of their respective Lodges. With every good wish,we are yours very fraternally, Josh. Mather, Pro. C.S. ; T.Baskerville. D.P.C.S. ; James Parr, Dis. Treas. ; Peter Edgar, Dig. Sec. The Lodge Examiners (Messrs. Lawrence Weir, and David Harris) after certifying on the very satisfactory condition of the books state that they regret very much to And in some of the Lodges, the very heavy sickness they have had to contend with during the past year which has deprived them of making the necessary gaits so many of them desire to see, but they trust that the coming year will bring better prosperity in that I direction.
