THE INFIRMARY.

===== 0002601_19060303_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
Increased Contributions from the Workingmen's Committee.

In the raising of funds on behalf of the Warrington Infirmary the Workingmen's Committee plays an important part. It is the ambition of the members to collect £l,OOO per annum for this most deserving institution, and during the past year they have come very near to the attainment of that ideal, the sum raised being £960. At the annual meeting of the Com- mittee, which was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall on Saturday afternoon an encouraging report was presented and the enthusiasm augured well for the success of the coming year's work. The Mayor (Alder- man Bolton) presided and amongst those present were : —Alderman Roberts (chairman of the committee), Rev. John Yonge, Councillor Dr. Joseph, Messrs. John Fair- dough, G. Thompson, J. H. J. Hampson (treasurer) and R. Ingham (secretary). Letters of apoloay for non- attendance were received from Mr. F. Monks, the Rev. 11. V. Pigot, and Mr. C. Jackson Holmes. The MAYOR said that was the third occasion on which he had had the pleasure of presiding at the annual meeting of the committee and each time the report appeared to be more favourable. He complimented the committee upon the fact that while the contributions exceeded last year's by £64 the expenses incurred were about £g less. That showed that they had a thoroughly conscien- tious and hard-working committee who were doing their utmost to aid a worthy institution—one of the best that they could possibly have in a town like Warrington. The Infirmary was of particular advantage to workingmen and it was managed as economically as possible.

===== 0002601_19060303_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ACCOUNTS.

Mr. HAMPSON said he was pleased to report a sub- stantial increase in the amounts received from the works which totalled £Bl4 is. ed. as against £750 148.2 d. last year. He was sorry that they had lost the help of two good firms owing to work closing down. The total amount raised during the year was £960 7s. 3d. as compared with £89645. 4d. After paying expenses the net sum handed over to the Infirmary was £945 9s. sd. He desired to thank the office staffs in the works for the trouble they had gone to in putting the contribu- tions through the wages books and all the delegates for the courtesy he had received when visiting the works. Alderman ROBERTS expressed the hope that as the result of the coming year's endeavours they would be able to hand over £l,OOO to the institution. He reminded the meeting that the Infirmary would soon have to be extended,and that would entail thousands of pounds of extra expenditure. The growth of the town demanded the increased accommodation, and he hoped the workingmen would make special efforts. For its size the Infirmary was second to none in efficiency in England. He hoped that when the Committee met next year the extension would be an accomplished fact. Since the Workingmen's Committee came into existence it had been instrumental in handing over to the Infirmary £14,325 14s. 4d. (Applause). Mr. E. HORROCKS, a new delegate, asked what amount the working people of Warrington were expected to subscribe and what the money was used for, The MAYOR said Alderman Roberts had expressed the hope that the committee would be able to raise £l,OOO. The money was all devoted to the expenses of the Infirmary and the working classes obtained the benefit. Mr. 1.. WADSWORTH moved the adoption of the balance sheet and said it was very gratifying to see the substantial increase in contributions. Much of the credit for this was due to Mr. Hammon who had intro- duced a better system of collection. Mr. C. H. HIBBERT seconded. He felt that £l,OOO was a very small amount for the committee to raise and he was sure they xould obtain it during the com- ing year. He was told that workingmen's committees in other towns of no greater population than Warring- ton raised much larger sums than the Warrington committee. In view of the extension which would soon have to be undertaken the committee ought to arrange for a systematic course of collection in all the works of the town and aim at raising £1,500. He firmly believed they could do that if they put their shoulders to the wheel. The balance sheet was adopted. THE COMMITTEE.

On the motion of Mr. WATKINS seconded by Mr. CLARKE it was decided that the delegates present should form a general committee with power to add to their number. ELECTION OF OFFICERS. Mr. G. THOMPSON said it bad been his pleasure on many occasions to propose the election of Alderman I Roberts as chairman of the committee. Owing to the death of Mr. T.Bi rchall, whose services to the Infirmary they all appreciated, a vacancy occurred on the Finance and Visiting Committee of the insti- tution. He (Mr. Thompson) thought it was only fair that the Workingmen's Committee should be represented and on his proposition Alderman Roberta was elected a member of that committee. He had now pleasure in moving that Alderman Roberts be re-elected chairman of the Workingmen's Committee to which he bad given many years of painstaking service. In order to show the need for funds Mr. Thompson pointed out that since 1901 there had been an increase of 461 accident and emergency cases annually dealt with and the total number of cases treated to-day was an increase of 2,118 over 1901. During the same period the annual cost of drugs had risen by £221, the expendi- ture under that head during the past year being nearly £BOO. It took the Finance Committee all their time to make both ends meet. _

MT. ECKERSLEY seconded and Mr. BROADMURST sup- ported the proposition which was unanimously carried. Mr. H. HOPE moved the re-election of Councillor Burton as deputy chairman and said they could not possibly have a better man for the office. Mr. JONES seconded and the motion was agreed to. Mr HORROCKS moved the re-election of Mr. R. Higham as secretary. He again asked how much the committee was expected to find for the Infirmary. Where did it begin and where did it end? The MATOR : It never ends. (Laughter.) Mr. HORROCKS : If it is never going to end it is no use beginning on those lines. Mr. A. WRITE seconded and said the success of the committee was largely due to the energy of the Secretary. The motion was carried and Mr. HIGRAM returned thanks. _ _

On the motion of Mr. TAYLOR, seconded by Mr. DAINTITII, Mr. J. H. J. Hampson was re-elected hon. treasurer. Alderman John Roberts, Councillor Burton and Messrs. C. M. Watkins and William Daintith were appointed members of the Board of Management, and Mr. George Walker was re-elected representative on the Education Committee.

Burning Fatality. Mr. S. Brighouse, County Coroner, held an inquest at the Police Station on Tuesday touching the death of Levi Bebbington (40), ironworker, 89, Pierpoint-street. —The evidence showed that at seven o'clock on Thursday morning of last week deceased was "knocked up" by a fellow-workman to go to work. He com- plained of feeling tired and told the man that he would not go to work that day. After dismissing the man deceased went into the kitchen and shortly afterwards his wife, who was in the front room, heard a shout. On going into the kitchen she saw deceased with his shirt on fire. She took up a rough apron and put out the flames but not before the deceased had been very severely burnt about the body. He was removed to the Infirmary where he died on the following day. He had told his wife that he took his pipe from the mantle- piece, lit it, and turned round, when the hack portion of his shirt caught flre.—A verdict of " Accidental death " was returned,

My Dear Children, I wonder how many of you have gardens of your own. During the past week I have been arranging for you to hold a flower show in the summer. a I propose to give you a list of the seeds required, and during the summer I shall award nice prizes for the best flowers sent from these seeds. All at them will be very easy to cultivate, and will thrive in the open ground. None must be grown in greenhouses, this would hardly be fair as all of you have not a greenhouse. I want you all to think over this proposition, and it you wish to enter for the competition, please send me in your names and addresses during the next week or two. in I was a young boy I used to grow flowers n a little garden of my own. and I sent them to several small flower shows, and I was fortunate enough to get several prizes. I have awarded a prize this week to Leonard Oram for the amusing piece of poetry printed further down these columns, I shall also be very pleased to send a prize to Nancy Porter for her interesting story, but unfortunately I have mis- laid her address, if she will kindly send me this, a prize will be forwarded at once. Many other pieces of poetry and short stories have been sent me this week, and I hope to print these and award the prizes in a week or two. Nine of my post card albums have been sent off this week, each album dispatched means twenty-four new name q for our League. You will see a very in- teresting letter from a new member living in Constantinople, I am sure you would all like to hear more about this interesting place, and I should be pleased to have a long letter telling me all about the habits and customs of the people of Constantinople. Perhaps this little girl will give me her lull address as I may at some future time award her a prize. With all good wishes from

UNCLE NED.

Constantinople, 3rd February, 1906. • Dear Uncle ted, You will perhaps be surprised to get a letter from a little girl in Constantinople. My father . gets an English paper sent to him, and I am very fond of reading the , Children's Corner, and I would like to become a member of the Children's Corner League. came out to Turkey with my mother three years ago, we came by sea, and when we came uto the•Bosphorus we thought it was a beautiful place, there are mosques and minarets on all sides. ;On the water there are lots off steamboats to carry passengers to the different towns and villages on the Bosphorus, and, Golden Horn. There are also hundreds of little native boats, and in one of these we landed and very glad we were to see dadda, who was waiting for us on the quay. •When we got on shore the Turkish police, who all wear swords and a great many carry pistols in their belts, took us to the Custom House to look at our passport and stamp it. Then they put our names in a book as new arrivals, and after looking into our luggage told us we could go. When the porters, or hamals as they are called, had finished shouting and quarrelling about the price to carry our luggage, it was taken through. the huilding and put into a carriage which we call an araba, and the coachman is called an arabadji, we entered another carriage and moved off as quickly as we could. The dogs are in every street—there are hun- dreds of them—and every one keeps to its own street. It a strange one comes in the others all begin to light him and drive him away unless he proves to u rest. e strong and can fight better than the They lay on the pavement and in the street and they Will not move If udo not shout " boost to them " hoos't " is what every one here shouts at the dogs to make them move away. Most of the dogs are very dirty as they live day and night in the streets and feed on the rubbish thrown out from the houses, but the puppies are pretty, fat little things. sometimes Ited some of these, and they always remember me. The streets are most of them very dirt* and a great many smell very badly. Many of the houses art built of wood, and lots look as if they would tumble down if you gave them a good hard push, but some of them have thick strong walls, and have been standing for huntheds of years. The streets are not like the English ones, /they are small and all ups and downs, but a few are very nice. I shall now be looking anxiously in the Child- ren's Corner to see if you would like to hear more about this wonderful old city of Constanti- nople. From your new little niece, ALICE LUCE HILDA UNSTEAD, (81).

I

Dear Uncle Ned, It gives me great pleasure to write these few lives to you On Friday morning when the ground was covered in snow, a beautiful book arrived for me. It is you that I have to thank for this present. The book is called " Torn Martin," and I was much taken up with the story. In i later years t x'ill always remind me of you and your nephews and nieces, With very great thanks, I still remain, yours affectionately, Wm. CLARKE.

Deavencle Ned, , Thank you very much for the nice book you sent me. I received it on Saturday. I have read it through, and think it is very nice. I' did not expect to get a prize, and when it came it nearly took my appetite away for tea. Your loving little nephew, SYDNEY ELLIOTT.

THE BRAVE FIREMAN. (Concluded).

"Mother, mother!" cried little Wile. "All right, don't be afraid," said the fireman, "I am taking you to your mother. Keep your mouth shut, and dont let the smoke in. Don't speak again either of you! " He held them tightly, one in each arm, and had not a hand free to feel his way. Nelly's arms clung round about his neck. She was too frightened to speak or move, but she was not afraid of the strange man any more. As the flames were coming up the stairs, the fire- man dared not run straight down, for fear the steps might be burnt through. So he tried each one very carefully, and moved down slowly. Sometimes the smoke and heat that were coming up were so great, that the fireman Was almost driven baek to try the window again. But as the flames had now burst out below the window, he could not get down the ladder with the children, and it was too far to throw them down even upon the carpets and beds kept ready to catch them. He must go on, as the only chance of escape is by the stairs. Nelly wondered if these could be the same stairs that she and Dolly had gone up all their lives. What could make them so long? She thought it must all be a dream, and that she should go on down, and down until she woke. Then she heard shouting and crying in the street. There seemed to be a great many people quite near, bat they were all outside. At last the fireman reached the front door, and as soon as the crowd saw him with the children in his arms, they cried out " The children are saved! All honour to thei brave firemen!" and someone ran out of the crowd, threw her arms round the children, and round the fireman too, and sobbed, but could not speak..,, Who was it? Why, their mother!

NANCY PORTgR.

Dear Uncle Ned, _ _ I am pleased to see what a large /number of• children have joined your League, and I think it has made great progress. lam sending you a piece of poetry. It is called GETTING INFORMATION OUT OF PA. My pa he didn't go down town Last evening atter tea, But got a book an' settled down • As comfy as could be. I'll tellsou I was offul glad To have my pa about, To answer all the things I had Been tryin' to find out. And so I asked him why the world Is round instead of square? And why the piggies tails are curled? And why don't fish breathe air? And why the moon don't hit the stars? And why the dark is black? And jest how manY\ birds there are? And will the wind- come back?

And why does water stay in wells? And why do June-bugs hum? And what s the roar I hear in shells?

And W-1-1-er;-rill-"&;.iiiii;;;;;;;F And why the grass is always green Instead of sometimes blue? And why a bean will grow a bean And nqt an apple too?

And why a horse can't learn to moo? And why a cow can't neigh? And do the fairies live on dew? And what makes hair turn grey? And then my pa got up an', Gee The ()Ifni words he said, I hadn't done a thing but he Jest sent me off to bed, I remain your affectionate nephew, LEONARD ()RAM.

THE BLIND BOY.

0 l say What is that thing call'd Light Which .1 must ne'er enjoy, What are the blessings of the sight, 0 tell your poor blind boy. You talk of wond'rous things you see, You say the sun shines bright, I feel him warm, but how can he Or make it slay or night. My day or night myself I make Whene'er I sleep or play; And could I ever keep awake, With me 'twere always day. BARRY E. CLARKE.

NORTHERN UNION LEAGUE.

RESULTS OF SATURDAY'S MATCHES.

G. M. P. G. T. P. Broughton R 2 4 16 *Widnes 0 0 0 *Hull Leigh 0 1 3 *Millom *Rochdale H 0 2 6 Bradford *Batley *St.Helens 2 1 7 Dewsbury *Swinton l2 8 Wigan - 01 3 *Leeds 3 3 15 York 3 2 12 *Barrow *Halifax 1 2 8 Oldham 2 0 4

Warrington Draw at Barrow. The Warrington men were expected to bring full points away from Barrow on Saturday, but they only succeeded in halving the spoil, the raatch ending in a draw. Dickinson re-appeared at centre for Warrington, while Brooks took the plaas of Kenyon on the left wing. Gifford was a prominent absentee from the home side. The weather was very had. Snow and sleet falling a good part of the time, and the ground was consequently very heavy. Warrington had the best of the game and should have won, but poor handling of the wet ball caused chances to be missed. The teams were :

Barrow—Spencer, Fox, Bowker, Mellor, Little ; Fallowileld, WlCenny, Treweek, Morgan, Lloyd, Brown, Brough, Jardine, Trescatherie, and Craig. Warrington—Berry, Brooks, Taylor, Dickinson, Widdeson, Hookenhall, Lees, Preston, Boardman, Thomas, Heath, Swift, Harmer, Jolley, and Morrison.* Referee, E. H. Smirk, Preston. Preston kicked, off, and Spencer replying to antre, Hockenhall was the first to open out the game from the initial scrimmage.Dickenson failed to hold the oval,and Warrington missed an opening in the first few minutes. The visiting backs again got their passing machinery at work only to find the movement break down at the right wing, and profiting by the inability of the " Wire- pullers" to cross the line, Fallowfield cleared to centre, where Berry shone in some huge touch kicking. A penalty against the visitors was taken by Bowker,but the kick went wide of the mark, and this narrow escape was succeeded by Hochenhall breaking away and racing down the centre. The home quarters were reached until Little bore down on the tricky half-back and the situa- tion was saved. Another battle royal was witnessed between the forwards, for after the Warrington set had dribbled dangerously near the home goal, the Barrow vanguard got the better of a hot scrimmage, and a long rush landed the game once more at neutral quarters. Here the home half-backs tried to open out the game, but the tackling of Taylor and Dickenson was too safe to allow of much progsess, whilst Bowker and Mellor • returned the compliment when Dicken- son accepted a pass from Lees, and tried to make headway. Penalties against Warrington assisted the Barrovians to ease the pressure slightly, and though the visitors claimed a big advantage forward, the Northerners stuck to their opponents tenaciously, the tackling being of a robust nature. Mellor broke away, and the chances favoured the locals, until Fox upset the movement with offside, play. Warrington retaliated and Widdeson was given a chance, but he preferred to kick and Spencer saved. Warrington rushed the scrimmages, and the home custodian was busily employed in defending until Bowker relieved, and Little following up, Berry was tackled in possession. However, Lees, Hockenhall, Dickenson and Widdeson combined neatly and Brooks made a big effort to get through. The kicking was sure and eventually Bowker and Berry excelled in some fine exchange kicking, the advantage resting with the visitors. Lees next made a fine attempt to cross, being pulled up only a few feet from the line. Barrow rushed I sway to the opposite end, where Berry was repeatedly called upon, and not found wanting. The visitors' backs were usually in possession and Brooks tried hard to get through, only to find Little ever on the alert and upset calculations. Bowker had another ineffectual shot at goal, and after this failure the Warrington forwaris asserted themselves splendidly, and Spencer was fully employed in arresting progress. A speedy retaliation was made by the home eight, and at half-time no substantial score had been recorded' The second portion was almost a repetition of the earlier stages, Hockenhall and Lee being well shadowed by McKenny and Fallowfleld. Preston had two fruit- less kicks at goals for the visitors, and Treweeke on one occasion saved the home line in splendid fashion. In the last minute Hockenhall, Lees and Taylor combined effectively, but Widdeson knockpd forward from the final pass, and a splendid chance was lost, the game end- thy in a pointless draw.

TUESDAY'S MATCHES.

Bradford Halifax 0 0 0 Wakefield _1 0 2

Batley 1 1 5 Barrow 0 0 ,0 lieighley —0 0 0 _ .

Warrington Beaten at Bradford. On Tuesday, Warrington departed for Bradford in the middle of 4 heavy snowstorm, and with several regular players absent. They found the Park Avenue ground covered with slush and almost unfit for play, while only about 2,000 spectators assembled. Mr. F. Kennedy, (Broughton) was referee and teams were as follows : Bradford—Gunn, back ; Dobson, Heseltine, Mosby, and Dechan, three-quarter backs ; Marsden and Sur- man, half backs ; Laidlaw, Feather, Smales, Grayson, Turner, Mann, Sharratt and Rees, forwards. Warrington—Knight, back ; Widdison, Dickinson, Taylor, and Brooks, three-quarter backs ; Lees and Hockenball, half backs ; Preston, Heath, Swift, Thomas, Harmer, Morrison, Foster, and Jolley, for- wards. . . .

Bradford were soon on the attack, but the viaitors by good use of their feet, and splendid kicking by Knight, for a long time kept the home team at bay. Gunn was first to make a notable individual effort by breaking his way through the thick of his opponents, and having made a fine opening, and got near the Warrington line, he passed to Feather. The home forward, however, could not hold the ball, and a try was thrown away. The visitors were now kept busy defending, and after a scrummage Marsden neatly dropped a goal. Not long after Warrington were penalised, and Marsden placed another goal. The next score was the result of more attractive work. Marsden got away near the centre after a scrummage and ran. Then he transferred to Heseltine, who out-witted his opponents, and finished a splendid run by making a try. Laidlaw made a good shot for goal, but the ball went slightly wide. At half- time the score was—Bradford 2 goals and 1 try (7 points), to Warrington, nil. The second half opened with dull play in neutral territory,the men finding it difficult to keep their feet. Again Knight distinguished himself by fine kicking, gaining a lot of ground thereby; but the forwards began to give way, and could not keep on the attack for long. More than once, however, Warrington looked like scoring. Marsden made a mistake, and the ball was rushed to near the home line. A penalty lost the visitors their position, but their forwards again became dangerous, and Bradford being penalised, Knight had a shot at goal. The ball went wide, but Brooks by smart following up, came near scoring, the ball just beating him in going dead. Close on the finish Bradford once more looked like adding to their points, but Knight was safe, so that the final read—Bradford 2 goals and 1 try (7 points), to Warrington nil.

FRIENDLY MATCH. _WARRINGTON "A' V. RUNCORN FERTILIZERS.

Played at Wilderspool before a capital attendance. Isherwood, the old Warrington centre, made a welcome re-appearance in tho team, after ten weeks' absence. The game was of a one-sided nature, though the visitors caused quite a surprise by opening the scoring, taking advantage of a mull by the home back,and registering a try. Warrington pressed continuously after and nally won by 53 points to Runcorn 8 points.

The Examiner, Saturday, March 3, 1906.

PADGATE.

SUNDAY SCHOOL SocIAL.—An enjoyable social, pro- moted by the teachers and elder scholars of Christ Church Sunday school, was held in the National School on Monday evening. About 120 persons were present and the proceedings were highly successful. Games, dancing and songs made up the programme, and refresh- ments were provided. Miss Muriel Mellor sang the songs " Dear old home" and" Navaho," and tor the latter she was encored, as was also Miss E. Croughton for her rendering of "The volunteer organist." Miss L. Ross sweetly sang "The star of Bethlehem," while Misses L. and E. Ross were heard to advantage in the duet, " When the lights are low," for which they were recalled. The pianoforte duet," Sleigh bells," was well played by Misses A. Heath and B. Horridge. Selections on the gramaphone were given by Mr. Smallman. Mr. G. Rigbey made an efficient M.C.

CHURCH On ENTERTAINED—The Clergy, wardens, sidesmen and choirruen of Christ Church were on Monday evening entertained to slipper at the Church House, Padgate by Mrs. Mather, wife of Mr. G.Mather, the people's warden. The lower room of the building was most tastefully decorated with plants and flowers. The repast was of an excellent character. A novel toast list was gone through. As both proposers and responders were called on impromptu some amusing speeches were given. The list included the usual loyal toasts and in addition there were "The Bishop and clergy," " Agriculture," "Trade and commerce of the neighbourhood," " Music," " Law "and the " Host and Hostess." In proposing the health of Mr. and Mrs. Mather, the Vicar spoke of the enthusiasm which Mr. Mather threw into all his public duties as evidenced by his attendances on the Rural District Council, the Board of Guardians and as chairman of the Cottage Homes Committee. The toast was received with acclamation, and Mr. Mater briefly replied. After the speechmaking the company retired to the supper roorn,where the remainder of the evening was pleasantly spent in billiards, whist and conversation.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

Religious Instruction. Sir,—l have read my friend the Rev. J. F.Matthews' address to the Warrington Young Free Churchmens' Auxilliary with interest. It is not necessary to dwell on points of agreement, which are many. I single out the point of difference so that by friendly discussion, if we cannot see eye to eye, we may at least be better able to appreciate each others point of view. Mr. Matthews reasons strongly foram elimination of all religious and Biblical teaching from state-supported schools, contending that religion is a spiritual thing concerning the Churches only, and that the secularizing of our schools is the most logical and consistent course. Stress is laid upon the fact that this would remove from the school sphere "sectarian controversy and bitterness." To controversy, so far as it is conducted in the proper spirit, there can be no objection, we can only arrive at the truest and best through it, but as for bitterness, why should this arise? I have found men of all creed (especially of late) discussing this difficult question in a spirit unmarred by any bitterness. But coming to the main point it has always seemed to me desirable in considering this problem to ask ourselves the question. Why did the State take over education ? Because it considered that an ignorant people were a national weakness and a danger to material well-being True, but not this only, a greater danger to the State lay in the possibility of an immoral people, ignorant of the moral principles upon which the well-being of society is based. It is far more important to the State that the people should be honest than that they should know the names of the countries in Europe, It is the violation of moral laws which cause the chief expense and the chief danger in an uneducated people. For a State to undertake education and then to eliminate from its curriculm the training of the most important, the moral faculties, appears to do Away with the princi- pal object for which education was nationalized. This step would be far more illogical than the position of those who seek a common religious basis from which to teach morals. I agree with Mr. Matthews that thy State has nothing to do with spiritual matters, if be this he means matters which concern the spiritual life and world only. But the State is deeply concerned that the children who are to be its future citizens should be rooted in the principles upon which, more than anything else, the stability of a State rests. It is of vital import- ance that such should be honest, truthful, loving and virtuous.

The question then arises is their a middle course between that advocated by Mr. Matthews viz., limiting State education to secular subjects, and that of giving a religious education as a foundation for a moral character which the State desires and demands for its own safety? Can we for example effectively teach children that they must not steal because the policeman may catch them,and they will be punished by the State? Can we make children honest by teaching them that honesty is the best policy? Can an ethical standard be established which has any restraining or inspiring force which does not rest upon the foundation of a Divine Governor of the universe, a law giver whose laws can never be transgrassed with imnunity ? lam aware that ethical truths are deemed sufficient by some, but these lack the potency, and authority required for general humanity. Abstract truth, especially to a child has little force unless it is embodied and unless it is made personal. But I will have the question asked as it has been asked hundreds of times. How are you going to draw the line between religious truths which may be deemed essential and are practically common to Chris- tians, and the beliefs which are denominational? I answer that I am not concerned about drawing lines of demarkations. The sun gilds the Eastern sky, morning is born, it is day. Who can tell the moment dividing the light from the darkness and yet no one doubts it was dark,and now it is light. The colours melt into each other inithe rainbow, we cannot draw dividing lines between these, and yet we clearly discern what is yellow and what is blue. In the same way we can clearly see that it is important to the State that children should be upright and honest, and it is a matter outside the interests of the State whether they are brought up as Calvanists or Arminians ; but we cannot readily determine the place of certain truths which lie in the intermediate territory, and may be included or excluded from the interests of the State. All we can do is to drawn line as clearly as we can by words through the centre of this inter- mediate sphere and trust the teachers to confine them- selves to their own territory. The small minority of parents who would object to this would be protected as heretofore by the Conscience Clause. The solution which Mr. Matthews cordially accepts appears to me to be about the worst possible. He ' would have secular education supplemented if de- manded by parents by denominational teaching on certain mornings of the week, to be given. I presume, by ministers of different denominations. I hold that the trained teachers are best able to give religious and all other teaching. Such religious teaching should not be limited to specifically religious subjects, it should permeate everything. For example in teaching secular history how important it is that the rise and fall of nations should be traced to the domin- ating causes, the obedience and the disobedience to the laws of the Eternal. Personally I consider denomina- tional teaching should be confined to the home, the Church, Chapel, and Sunday school, and that it is out of place in State schools, and will have a tendency to undermine the authority of the teachers, and confuse the minds of the children ; but if any large number of parents demand it, it seems equitable that their wishes should be favourably considered. I trust however Nonconformists will take no part in it. CHARLES E. PARRER.

TIIE REPORT.

ME

A

In their thirty-ninth annual report, the Directors stated that the nett profit on the year's working amounted to £948 18s. 9d., out of which the directors proposed to pay a dividend of £4 per cent, and a bonus of Is. per share (free of income tax), which would amount to £877 9s. 7d., leaving a balance to be carried to undivided profit of £7l Os. 2d., and making a total undivided profit of .C 1,688 10s. 11d. Three of the properties in possession had been sold and a nett profit realised of £2Bl 12s. Bd., which amount had been carried

to property suspense account. The retiring directors were Messrs. S. %V. Dallow, %V. J. Shaw, and F. Timper- ley, who were eligible for re-election. The CHAIRMAN, having expressed regret at the illness of Mr. A. H. Clive, said the Directors were glad to be able to present a favourable balance sheet. They felt they could come before the members with confidence. They had made no rash promises, but they had done their best in the interests of everybody concerned. He moved the adoption of the balance,sheet. Mr. JOSEPTI TAYLOR seconded.

The CHAIRMAN observed that there was not a single member on notice of withdrawal. They had paid every- one off and had a clear book—a fact which spoke well for the past year's working of the society. A gentleman who said 'he spoke on behalf of Mr. Thomas Harrison, a member, who was bedfast, said Mr. Harrison had told him he had applied several times for his money but could not get it though he had received several instalments. The CHAIRMAN said the society had not a sin,de person on the list who was not paid oil. Evidently Mr. Harrison had not put the notice in proper form. If he was on notice he would be paid off immediately. The Board would go into the matter at their next mooting. Mr. W. J. SHAN/ said there had been several instances in years gone by where people had come to the office monthly or weekly and obtained instalments. The Society had paid over £2,000 in with- drawals, the amount in some cases being as much as £lOO, and it was not likely they would refuse a small sum like £4 145., which was the amount in the case called attention to. TOO CHAIRMAN said that if Mr. Harrison would give notice ha could give him his word that he would be paid soon after the next Board meeting. Mr. F. SHAW asked if the Board would take the meet- ing into their confidence regarding the £2Bl which had been put to property suspense account, and give them some idea as to what they were going to do with it. He thoaght the shareholders might be taken into the consideration of the Board and that if there was any ' profit made it should be divided amongst the members The CHAIRMAN said the Board discussed that point for an hour and a half and came to the conclusion that it was the wisest course to put the money to a Property' Suspense Account, in case there were properties which ' would not make the amounts put against them and they would want something to meet that. Had the money been divided it would only have meant about Is. or , Is. 6d. for each shareholder. The accounts were then nnanimously approved. ELECTIONS. On the motion of Mr. JOSEPH TAYLOR seconded by Mr. A. COOK the retiring directors (Messrs. S. W. I Dallow, F. Timperley and W. J. Shaw) were re-elected, I so that the Board is constituted as follows :—Messrs. T. Critchley, .1. Cooper, C. H. Hibbert, T. Ince, J. Potter, W.J. Shaw, R. Shackleton and F. Timperley. The following were also re-elected :—Treasurer, Mr. Aquila Cook ; surveyor, Mr. William Twiss ; auditors,l Messrs, Edwin Bradshaw and W. H. Woodcock. On the motion of Mr. F. J.LoNGSRANV, seconded by Mr. TAYLOR, the directors and officials were accorded a hearty vote of thanks for their services during the year. At a subsequent meeting of the directors Mr. W. ! J. Shaw was elected president and Mr. Critcbley vice-. president.

HYMN STER AND POLITICIAN

AMUSING SATIRE OF MR. R. A NAYLOR.

Mr. Spencer Leigh Hughes, ("Sub Rosa ") has been having another tilt at Mr. R. A. Naylor in the columns of the" Morning Leader." He says : " I have been reading in the e Manchester Guardian ' a report of a meeting at Bangor, at which Mr. R. A. Naylor, the unsuccessful candidate for the Carnarvon Boroughs, made a speech about his recent experiences. The chairman, Mr. H. C. Vincent, reminded the audience that Mr. Naylor had been opposed to Mr. Lloyd George, one of the most brilliant debaters in the House of Commons, who had around him the glamor of his newly-won and richly deserved honors. This friendly recognition of the ability of an opponent does not seem to have pleased Mr. Naylor, for be remarked : _

_ The chairman has called my opponent one of the most brilliant debaters in the House of Commons. Well, the chairman may think that, but the House of Commons would differ from him. Of course, I have no right to say what opinion the House of Commons entertains on this point—nor has Mr. Naylor—but we will say that, in Mr. Navlor's absence, Mr. Lloyd-George has managed to attract some attention on the floor.

It seems to me that Mr. Naylor makee a tactical mis- take in belittling his opponent's gifts, for there is less discredit in being beaten by an able man than by one who is not able. However, that is Mr. Naylor's business, and he is entitled to his own opinion. His speech is marked by subtlety of thought in some places, by confusion of thought in others—and now and then by an absence of thought. There is subtlety in this : The Opposition tells me that I have lost. I ask them how I could have lost. If you never had a thing you could not lose it. From this I am tempted to suppose that Mr. Naylor is a pure Balfourian.

A little later, however, confusion of thought appears, for Mr. Naylor remarked, in regard to the Liberals in the Carnarron Boroughs—" they have certainly gained a seat." But they have had the seat before, and if you cannot lose that which you never had, you cannot gain that which you previously possessed. In the constituency in question, they have maintained the status quo ante. It is a case of" as you were," and a case of as they are likely to be for a long time. If Mr. Nayl')r is satisfied with the reflection that he did not lose, no one else is likely to grumble.

The part of the speech which interests me far more than any other is that in which Mr. Naylor says: Well, we have had a lot of hymn-singing, and if I must tell you the truth, I think you sing rather too much. This may or may not ben fair comment—l express no opinion—but I am amazed to find Mr. Naylor rnaking such a remark. He is one of the most prolific hymn- writers of the day, and I remember that when I first saw his volume—perhaps I ought to say one of his volumes—of devotional verse, it struck me that scrme of the hymns might have a sad, not to say a grim, significance after the election. The event has shown that my opinion was not ill-founded.

Here, for example, is a verse which no doubt expresses absolutely the feelings of a candidate who, after making many speeches to attentive audiences, is, after all, rejected : For just to simply listen, Without more special heed, Seen afterwards in actions, Is foolishness indeed.

lam sure that Mr. Naylor was firmly convinced of the truth of that remark when he first made it, but to-day he probably is even more decidedly of opinion that the conduct indicated is foolishness indeed. When I first reviewed these tender and improving hymns or spiritual songs, I hinted that perhaps the follo-ving lines might be chanted appropriately after the declaration of the poll: How sad the closing of the day When we have nothing done. And here again my apprehensive, and even gloomy, prognostications have been fulfilled.

In this Bangor speech I find allusions to " false practices " on the part of some of the electors or workers in the Carnarvon Boroughs. if he entertains such views, he may possibly derive consolation from this telling stanza, the product of his own pen: Beware of adulation, And do not stand among The double-hearted people, Who flatter with the tongue; Yield not then to these charmers, For better blame severe And friendly admonition, Than a deceitful cheer. That last line is likely to recur to his memory when he thinks of the loud cheers during which he so often resumed his seat, and the discouraging result of the election. There may be a hidden meaning in this verse—a verse originally written with reference to children: Then as they grow to man's estate, They bless you while you stay, But they arise and bless you more When you have gone away. I can imagine a wealthy and defeated candidate being haunted by a horrible suspicion that these words cor- rectly describe the attitude of agents and workers in the constituency which has rejected him.

There is another hymn in this choice collection which happily indicates the difference between the joyous confidence with which a political champion prances into the fray, and his chastened demeanour when, though he may argue that he has not lost, he has to acknowledge that he has not gained. The hymn begins in this way: With joy we look and see the verdant grass, The lovely flowers so gay, but Ah! alas! Their beauty fades, and they cease to adorn, To-day cut down, and withered in the morn. It is even so in regard to the candidate who night after night repeats the comforting message," Work will win," who has countless votes of confidence carried unanimously in his favor, whose ears are continually assailed by a choral assurance that he is a jolly good fellow—and who then finds that, so far as the House of Commons is con- cerned, he is not in it. I can imagine such a man repeating those words, "Ah ! alas ! " with deep emotion.

-- Towards the end of his speech Mr. Naylor informed his Bangor audience : If you want me to come here again, and you send me the invitation, it will be received with the

greatest consideration. This frank intimation, of the nature of a broad hint, does not appear to have evoked any response one way or the other. During the period of doubt while await- ing the suggested invitation, Mr. Naylor will do well to remember these cheery words taken from another of his hymns : Strive on and struggle still, Do not too sadly grieve. Here we have the " Nil desperandum " or " Never say die," tone which should distinguish all the utterances of the fighting politician. And, after all, Mr. Naylor should remember that others beside himself fell in the fray. He must have foreseen the defeat of Mr. Balfour and of many another ex-Cabinet Minister, when he wrote :

Perfection seems so far away, And distant from the best of men, That panting hearts will often say, Why slaonld we poor ones try so when The foremost champions so fail —"When they the heights attempt to scale? The question comes most reasonably from a man in his position. Mr. Naylor is probably not the only man whose panting heart has been asking of late why the poor ones, the rank and file, should make such efforts when the foremost champions were so conspicu- ously unsuccessful. But I cannot understand why he should complain that the Welsh sing too many hymns, unless those abandoned Carnarvon people not only voted against him, but also chose the hymns of other authors. S. L. IL

ELECTIVE AUDITORS ELECTION.

Close Fight for Second Place.

The election of two elective auditors for the Borough took place on Thursday but so quiet were the proceed- ings that Warringtonians were hardly aware that an election was in progress. This apparent lack of interest was due no doubt to the almost complete absence of mural literature, the only notification in this direction of the presence of candidates:in the field being a number of postcards sent out in support of Mr. L. Bolton and Mr. E. Bradshaw, the retiring auditors. This arose from the decision of the Lord Chief Justice and Mr. Justice Channel! who held that section 5 of the Municipal Elections Corrupt and Illegal Practices Act of 18sa meant that,while intending electors could spend money during their contest, aldermen and elective auditors could not without rendering themselves liable to a fine of £lOO and five years disfranchisement. In former years handbills &c., have been pretty freely dis- tributed and as, this year, addresses were issued only on behalf of the retiring auditors many of the electors were not aware of the presence of a third candidate in the person of Mr. Ebenezer England. The new comer however, was backed by a number of willing workers from the ranks of the Liberal and Labour Parties while the other two gentlemen also received sectional support Mr. Bolton's claims being strongly advocated by mem- bers of the Non-Trading Association. Polling took place at the Gymnasium on Thursday and throughout the day was very slow only 10 per cent. of the burgesses recording their votes. The poll closed at eight o'clock when the ballot boxes were conveyed to the Town Hall, where the votes were counted, and originally resulted as follows :—Bolton 479, England 321, Bradshaw 319. Two papers not being accounted for, the Mayor,in view of the narrow majority in favour of Mr. England at once ordered a recount which resulted in two more votes being added for Mr. Bolton the figures being : Bolton 4Bl England Bradshaw

Mr. BRADSHAW and Mr. ENGLAND both called for a further re-count, but the figures remained unaltered, the first two named being elected. Nine votes were spoiled by the electors recording their crosses for two candidates, whereas they were only entitled to vote for one.

===== 0002601_19060303_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME SPEECH-MAKING.

Mr. LAWRENCE BOLTON moved a vote of thanks to the Mayor for his services as retarning officer and to the officers.

Mr. EBENEZER ExGL A.ND seconded. He sympathised, he said, with his friend Mr. Bradshaw, in his defeat, as he felt he was a most able man for the elective auditor- ship. The Mayor's duties had not been very arduous, but he had carried them out in a =tuner which showed that whateverlhe undertook!he endeavoured to carryout to the best of his ability. He desired to thank his per- sonal friends as well as his political friends—Liberal and Labour—for their work on his behalf. Although nOne of the candidates were officially nominated by any party they were all supported by sections, and he thought it only right that those sections should be thanked.

Mr. BRA.DsHAw supported the motion. He hoped he was not so ungenerous as to begrudge the other two gentlemen their victories. Personally he gave thorn his heartiest congratulations. He had always endeav- oured to serve the town to the best of his ability, and perhaps some day, if not as elective auditor, he would again appear before the electors. (Hoar, hear). The vote was heartily accorded, and a brief reply by the MAvou terminated the proceedings. Outside the Town Hall very few persons had assembled to hear the result and no official announcement being made those waiting were acquainted with the figures by the Rev. H. Dumuco. MAYOR'S AUBITOR. On Thursday Alderman James Fairclough was appointed Mayor's auditor, a position which he has held for a number of years, but this morning he resigned the office.

WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL The Proposed New Street.

LOCAL GOVERNMENT BOARD AND THE CORPORATION.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Town Council will be held on Tuesday when the following amongst other items will be considered :

SUNDAY TRADING. At a meeting of the Watch Committee a further letter from the Tradesmen's Association with reference to Sunday trading was read, and the Chief Constable sub- mitted a report thereon. ft was resolved that the report be printed,and copies be sent to the Tradesmen's Association with an intimation that if after perusing the report they still desire an interview, the Committee will be pleased to grant it.

===== 0002601_19060303_art0220.txt | included=true | included =====
PAYINIENT- OF EDUCATION GRANTS.

The Town Clerk reported at a meeting of the Finance and Estate Committee that the Board of Education pro- posed to pay the annual grant in respect af each school in one sum at the end of each school year, in substitu- tion for the present arrangement. It was resolved that the Town Clerk be instructed to communicate with the Treasurer of the Board of Education urging upon him the necessity of paying the annual grant by Instalments, as the proposed new system would seriously increase the burden of local elementary education.

THE PROPOSED NEW STREET. LOCAL GOVERNMENT HOARD AND THE CORPORATION'S •"-v 'll7 APPLICATION. At a meeting of the Finance and Estate Committee a letter from Mr. Noel T. Kershaw, on behalf of the i Local Government Board, was read n reference to the application of the Corporation to borrow the sum of £7,483 to defray expenditure in connection with the widening of streets as authorised by the Warrington Corporation Act,tlB99. The letter stated : "The Board are advised that the Act of 1899 cannot be regarded as authorising the construction of the new street at the back of Bridge-street, and that, moreover, the work appears to be outside the powers of the Town Council under the Public Health Act, 1875. The Board are thereforelnot prepared tdsanction the borrowing of the Sum of £654, the purchase money of the additional strip of land acquired from Mr. Garnett, and £127 for work done by the Highway Department in connec- tion with such new street. The Board, further, do not consider the item of £37 55., the costs incurred in negotiations (afterwards abandoned) for the acquisition of Trinity Church, to be properly chargeable to a loan account. The Board's formal sanctions to the borrowing of the sums of £4,876 and .£1,779 are forwarded to you here- with. _ _

The Town Clerk drew attention to the Board's remarks in reference to the land acquired from Mr. • Garnett, and pointed out that several of the properties acquired from Mr, Garnett had been scheduled under the Act of 1899, and therefore a proportion of the above-named amount, in his opinion, ought to be in- cluded in the Bridge-street Improvement. It was resolved that the Town Clerk take the matter up with the 1,,0cal Government Board.

OVERSEERS.

The Finance and Estate Committee at their meeting resolved that Messrs. James Fairclough, Arthur Bennett, John Wilkinson, and James Grica Craik be appointed overseers for the ensuing year.

• STREET PAVING. The Town Clerk read the following resulution which he had received from the Edacation Committee at a meeting of the Paving and Sewerage Committee : " That the Paving and Sewerage Committee be recom- mended to pave the streets surrounding the Beamont' council school with wooden setts," and after consider- ation it was resolved that this committee only deem it advisible that O'Leary-street, being the street along the front of the school, be paved with wooden setts, GA RDEN ALLOTMENTS.

At a meeting of the Gas Committee it was resolved that the engineer be instructed to let the waste land round the Longford gas works for the purpose of garden allotments.

THE "WATER LILY."

The Water Committee recommend that prices be obtained by advertisement for the dismantling of the " Water Lily" engine at Winwick pumping station, and after dismantling that all firms sending in tenders for the work be requested to give an estimate for repair- ing in accordance with specification to be prepared by the water engineer._

ILECTRWITY AND TRAMWAYS. At a meeting of the Electricity and Tramways Com- mittee the Town Clerk reported that according to the instructions of the committee he had agreed with the London and North-Western Railway Company for the maintenance and periodical renewal of the Wilderspool level crossing for the sum of £5O. It was resolved that the action of the Town Clerk be approved.

-- -- The Engineer reported that he had received the sanc- tion of the Board of Trade to the scheme for supplying electricity at high pressure to Messrs. Naylor's Lay-bye. It was resolved that he be authorised to advertise for the necessary cables, and to invite tenders for the erec- tion of a sub-station in Greenall's Avenue.

EDUCATION. The General Purposes Committee at their meeting resolved that the following members be appointed a snb-committee to consider and report upon the advis- ability of amending the Education scheme :—Aldermen Bennett, Smethurst and Tinnion. Councillors Evans, Fulton-Smith and Pemberton. _ . _

It was also resolved that the Friendly Societies be requested to send delegates (in the proportion of one delegate to every 250 members) to a meeting at the Town Hall in order that they may select their repre- sentative on the Education Committee for recommend- ation to the Council.

. .

Following the lines of last year's work, the Warring- ton Musical Society wound up its season's activities on Tuesday evening with the performance of a miscellaneous r programme which proved every bit as enjoyable as its last year's counterpart. Would that the success of the concert with the public were equal to its merits,—a beggarly array of empty seats testifying to that lamentable apathy which has unfortunately attended all such enterprises in recent years. Excepting the violin soloist and some usual extensions of the orchestra the performance of the whole of the programme must be credited to the society's members—a fact of which they / may reasonably feel very proud, remembering the high standard of much that has been done at these concerts during the past decade—for right worthily was the society's reputation sustained. The chief choral item was Stanford's "The Revenge" heard now for the second time after a long interval. Whether the repetition marks any advance one need not enquire. Suffice it that this graphically picturesque work received, on the present occasion, a most intelli- / gent and fitting interpretation. As a musical composi- tion it bristles with difficulties and abounds in pitfalls, and never have members one and all so signally triumphed over them. At certain points the odds were I greatly in favour of an expected stumbling. That the 'expected did not happen is therefore in itself no small praise. But there were more positive and tangible merits than this in Tuesday evening's singing. In the varied moods of Tennyson's verse and in the kaleidoscopic changes of Stanford's musical treatment there is a fine field for the display of taste and expression, and here the more solidly praiseworthy features of the society's choral singing are to be found. The depicting of the sea-fight and the "battle-thunder," the dirge-like pass- ages following upon scenes of warfare and bloodshed, the devotional unaccompanied chorale, the stately " subject" in minuet tempo, the concluding pictures of heroic death, disaster and shipwreck: all these found strenuous work for the various sections of the voices worthy of the doing; the due performance of which carried with it is own reward in the building up of an impressive tone-picture. The declamation of much of the story, falling as it does, somewhat disproportionately, to the lot of the tenors, was remarkably good. An admirable promptness in the picking up of disjointed and often very nnvocal passages and an all-round excellence in "the attack" were also pleasingly conspicuous. The rendering of some part. songs confirmed in a general way this appreciation of the efficiency of the chorus, though they called for more subtle qualities in expression. In "The sea hath its pearls" I a truer sotto voce on the part of)ill concerned,and a better ' quality of tone in the tenors in prominent places near the close, were things to be desired, while similarly in the singing of the four-part "Love Song "(Bralem,s), in- creased piquancy and daintiness were well worth striving for. And why, having the support of the pianoforte accompaniment in "Orpheus with his lute " (German), should the ladies, who have this beautiful thing all to themselves, show a tendency, as they did more than , half-way through, to get out of tune ? But, after all, I those blemishes were negligible in the sense of seriously interfering with one's enjoyment of the pieces in which a critical ear might locate them, and much remains that redounds greatly to the credit of the Musical Society's members.

6.30,

Three ladies of the society's chorus greatly dis- tinguished themselves as solo vocalists. Miss Nellie Anderton is to be warmly congratulated upon a soulful interpretation of Brehm's " Rhapsodie" and upon scoring a sucess which is as gratifying as it is full of encouraging promise for her future. The rich orchestral parts were splendidly played, but in the male-voice chorus a more subdued volui.ne of tone would, we venture to think, have materially assisted the soloist and at the same time greatly enhanced the charming ensemble of this entrancing composition. Mrs. Arthur Booth never sang better than she did on this occasion. Certainly we do not remember to have heard her deal with an operatic excerpt in so artistic a way as she dealt with "Softly sighs " on Tuesday evening. Nor was a discriminating audience slow in noting the improvement,and in shewing its appreciation of the excel- lence of the performance. Responding to a recall Mrs Booth sang a sweet marceau called "Summer and Winter " from the pen of Mr. W. H. Payton, MO9. Bac. the society's capable and well-known organist and accompanist. Of Miss Marie Houghton's incursion into the region of operatic bravura," Qui la voce," it may be said that the discovery of such precocity is as startling as it is agreeable,aud serves to show how little one suspects the existence of talent hidden, as it were, near one's own doorstep. While admiring and fully ap- preciating the undoubted merit of Miss Houghton's vocalisation of Bellini's exacting roulades—a success emphasised by a rousing encore—a word of caution may be pardoned as to the propriety, in the young lady's interests, of safeguarding her future by directing her public singing into less ambitious channels during a period &Toted to close study and training. That such a word is necessary is seen in the tendency to a certain slip-shod manner of singing several cadences and in the imperfect "phrasing " of some other passages. The orchestral pieces and the solo work of Mr. Louis Wolff bulked largely in the programme. The line overture to "Richard III" ((;erman), and the "Casse Noisette" suite, given last year and now repeated by request, were on the whole exceedingly well played; only in that crucial test of the executant ability of the , violins—the first movement of the suite—was thereany- thing to note in the way of serious shortcomings. The G minor Max Bruch Concerto received a splendid interpretation by both principal . and orchostra—the treatment of the slow movement, with its most delicate pianissimos, being really superb. In Wieniawski's "Faust" Fastasia, Me. Wolff was most capably assisted at the pianoforte by Miss Pearse. But though a remark- able exhibition of finished technique, the piece was unconscionably prolonged, and it consequently deferred the conclusion of the concert to a late hour. Arr. F. El. Crossley discharged the very arduous task of conducting with over-conspicuous tact and skill.

CO-OPERATIVE CONFERENCE.

Successful Gathering at Warrington. A well-attended conference in connection with the Cheshire and North-Wales District Co-operative Asso- ciation was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey- , street, on Saturday afternoon. Mr. T. Critchley presi- dent of the Warrington Society, presided, and delegates were present from all the leading societies included in the district. Mr. CRITRHLEY, in a brief address, emphasized the growth of the Co-operative moment in Warrington, which was, he said, largely due to the excellent work done by their manager. Mr. Rathbone. With regard to Mr. Rathbone he would like to correct a wrong impression which had got abroad. When the Society gave him a present of 300 guineas, in recognition of his valuable services, the opinion was created that Mr. Rathbone was about to retire, but he was pleased to state that they hoped to retain his ser- vices for some time to come. (Applause.) Questions bearing upon the work of co-operative were asked by several delegates, satisfactory answers being given. ARE PRESENT-DA Y CO-OPERATORS PIONEERLIKE ? An interesting paper based on the above question was read by Mr. Dudley, president of the Runcorn Co-operative Society, who said:—The pioneers may be said to have set out on their mission with a lesser and a greater creed. We needonly concern ourselves to-day with what may be termed the lesser creed: (1) To establish a system of distribution. (2) To build, purchase, or erect houses in which those members desiring to assist one another in improving their domestic and social conditions may reside. (3) To manufacture such articles as the society may determine upon for the employment of such members as may be without employment. (4) To purchase or rent estates of land which may be cultivated by the members out of employment. (5) To educate and develop the intellect of the members, and for this purpose, 2i per cent, of the profits were set aside. Are we nearer the realisation ? Knowing that our doors are open to all comers, do we, in actual practice, render every possible assistance to the spread of the co-operative gospel? If our move- ment is a right principle, surely it must be made known broadcast, and the trend of its mission hung on the outer walls. But how can this be done without our educational agency? The very best and most capable men ani women of our movement should find a seat on the educational committee by reason of the fact that the most attractive and very best , form of co-operative truth should be arranged. !I must confess that my own experience points me to the fact that we have much leeway to make up in regard to our own co-operative faith and practice, and that this must receive first and paramount consideration. Then comes the question of distribution. We have always been most successful as a whole in distribution; but there are those on our management boards who will lag behind. We require to risk ourselves are we in all the branches of trade we ought to be? Are our premises as " taking " as they ought to be in attaction to the customer ? What we retail and ti!sm: we retail it is an important item, and vast opportunities lie near at hand. Then, as to departmental enterprize. Should we allow our individual consideration or regard for some person or persons in a certain line of business to deter us from advancing the establithment of such department in our own movement? No ; we do not desire to unneces- sarily hurt a single individual trader, and would rather let them alone •; but whatever may be our individual preferences, these great, sweeping social forces take I !little heed of the snug prosperity of the individual. Do not let us then here attempt to capture or cripple the best efforts of our movement in trying to prevent a real and legitimate development, for all is opened to us. This brings me to the housing question. How many times has our despised dividend enabled many scores of families to live in a better and more sanitary dwelling ! Further, does not our surplus capital open out to us a charming field by reason of it enabling us to invest in fully-equipped, modern property for the collective good of our members ? Look at the vast work done by the Woolwich and other societies in this respect, and again look at the excellent work executed by the Ealing Tenants Limited and such institutions.,

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Waring ("The aim of the class meeting ") a,:d Mr. 13.• H. Posnett, Runcorn ("The story of my class.") The Rev. T. G. HARTLEY again presided in the even- ing when there was a representative attendance. The Rev. H. H. GOWER secretary of the Liverpool district of the Class Leaders Association explained the object of the conference, There had been members and ; even ministers of the Wesleyan Church who had talked down the class meeting rather than back it up,but he was glad to believe that one of the indications of the growth of the spiritual life of their Church was to ba seen in the renewed interest in the class meeting. The CH A:ERMAN referred to the class meeting as being . an essential part of Methodist—an institution without which Methodism would die. If they banished from Methodism the idea contained in the class meeting of Christian fellowship they would cease to be Methodists. As Methodists they thought of their privileges, which were regarded by some Christian Churches with almost envious eyes, which had been handed down by their fathers—manifestations of real good fellowship, com- munion and friendliness—and which they had kept up and meant to keep up in the days which were to come. Goodfellowship was the foundation of their church life and he rejoiced to think that there was no likelihood of them departing from that idea. Such conventions as that were being held in order that they might help one another in a most important, serious, and, he was afraid, sometimes burdensome work. Although some people were inclined to speak slightingly of the junior class meetings he valued them very highly and rejoiced to think that they were a power for good. He was a great believer in the possibilities of life membership of a church. If they reared their children in the idea of life membership later in life it would not be a question of their joining the ehurch but as to whether they should leave. If 'their children joined the church when they were such they would remain with it, and would be true to its tr aditious, and would become princes in her future history. (Applause.) Interesting addresses were delivered by Miss A. IL. Kidd, Birkenhead, the Rev. 4V. IL Maltby, and Mr. A. J. Jones, of Bootle.

-- Junior Liberal Association. At the weekly meeting of this Association held at Gregory's Ca Buttermarket-street, on Monday evening three interesting biographies of eminent men were submitted by members of the biography committee —Mr. W. Williams read a paper on "Lord Granville " who, he said, filled with great distinction some of the highest offices of the State and by his tact and persuasive power was able to carry measures through the House of Lords which would have been rejected if piloted by any otber man.—A brief biography of "Mr J. Passmore Edwards "Was submitted by Mr. J. Hawnorn who pointed out that the subject of the paper was a successful newspaper man still amongst us whoa having gathered finely of this world'a wealth, had given the evening of his days a golden setting by a liberal-handed and intelligent philanthropy, which had brightened and sweetened the lives of many of his less favoured countrymen and women.—Mr. Ford read a paper on " Richard Cobden " and referred par- ticularly to the Free Trade struggle in which this great man played such a noble part. Cobden was the representative of the highest statesmanship and the true ideal of what a statesman ought to be. Living in constant and active intercourse with his fellowmen for 30 years, he was never convicted and never suspected of trick or subterfuge, or hidden design. He was transparently pure. He laboured purely for the common good, and sacrificed a leading commercial position to become useful to his countrymen.--A dis- cussion followed.—On the motion of Mr. A. H. Rathbone, seconded by Mr. Darbyshire, a vote of thanks was passed to the essayists.—The meeting w as presided over by Mr. H. W. Guest.

Little Miss Muffit sits on a tuffit But does not eat curds and whey, For she wiser has grown Since Tan Houten's' she'd known And now enjoys Cocoa each day, And the horridest spider That sat down beside her Could not send her From Mast away I No. 8.

demand for the little pearl shirt buttons this spring and coming summer all the newest lingerie blouses and shirts being trimmed with them. Linen gowns too, both plain and embroidered, that have been built for Egypt and the Riviera show rows of these homely little buttons down the fronts of the bodice and on the cuffs. Two years ago, the American craze for the large pearl button sent the price of the best shells up to £3OO a ton, and doubtless the traders are looking forward to reaping a harvest out of us this year. " The Smiths of Sorbiton."—lt is certainly true that no strangely beautiful women, no extrardinarily I handsome men, no hairbreadth escapes, no deeds of heroism, no frantically clever children, of the people ;of title—as Mr. Keble Howard carefully points out in the preface—find a place in this, his latest book, which ,by the way is already in its fifth edition. It is just a simple story of everyday suburban life, yet there is not one dull page from cover to cover, and one follows the , Smiths from the moment of their return from their honeymoon, to contented old age, with the keenest delight and intereSt. Decidedly the "Smiths of Surbi- , ton (Chapman and Hall) is among the Spring novels that should not be missed. EDITORIAL Prenxsm. (From tho Warrington Examiner, Fekruary 10th, 190 G. - Ebe Butibing " Architecturally the building is a distinct ornament to the street, and can be classed amongst the most striking erections in the town. It has been effectively carried out in the French Renaissance style, and the whole of the front is faced with glazed terra cotta: The premises are thoroughly adapted to the firm's rapidly increasing require- ments, and are fitted up in the most modern style. The cased-in windows are quite a new departure in Waitington. the glass-work at the back of each bearing the Warrington Borough Arms and the Manchester City Arms respectively. The electroliers with which the windows and the whole of the establishment are illuminated in-dce a very fine display." Ebe Zuqints6 : "It is certainly an advantage that the various sections of the firm's business can be carried out in one building under the supervision of the local inanager ('Jr. Harvey.) Hitherto the firoll has confined its attentions chiefly to gent's tailoring, for which it has achieved a high reputation, but in the new shop a special feature is the ladies depart- ment. A handsome staircase leads to the lofty and splendidly arranged show-room for ladies' costumes, and adjoining it is a fitting-room." =anitarp traorittibopq : "The workshop is one of the best adapted in the north of England, and the heating and ventilation arrangements are all that could be desired. The decorations of the interior are in perfect taste, and the electric fittings tire on an elaborate scale." Yet we are not satisfied.
